<meta>This is the title page and front binding of a 1530 Greek manuscript volume containing works by Hermes Trismegistus, Hero of Alexandria, and Ocellus Lucanus. The binding indicates it was once part of the royal library of King Francis I of France.</meta>

<note>The front cover of a 16th-century leather binding is decorated with intricate gold tooling. The border features a recurring motif of the crowned letter "F" and fleurs-de-lis, symbols of the French monarchy. In the center, a large oval cartouche contains several heraldic devices belonging to King Francis I: at the top is a salamander surrounded by flames, which was the King's personal emblem signifying his motto "I nourish and I extinguish"; below it is a crowned shield bearing three fleurs-de-lis, representing the French royal arms, encircled by the collar of the Order of Saint Michael. Two additional crowned "F" monograms flank the central shield. Above the central cartouche, a title is stamped in gold Greek capital letters.</note>

***

### **About this Collection**

It was common in the Renaissance to bind multiple distinct works into a single volume. This manuscript brings together the mechanical ingenuity of **Heron of Alexandria** with the mystical philosophy of **Hermeticism**. 

The inclusion of the *Poimandres* alongside a technical manual on pneumatics reflects the Renaissance worldview, which saw no conflict between the study of physical machinery and the exploration of divine or hidden wisdom. Both were considered essential branches of knowledge for a learned scholar or a royal patron like King Francis I.

<summary>This flyleaf provides a brief table of contents for the manuscript, identifying works by the engineer Heron of Alexandria and the mystical philosopher Hermes Trismegistus. It illustrates the Renaissance practice of binding scientific and philosophical texts together in a single prestigious volume.</summary>

<keywords>Heron of Alexandria, Poimandres, Hermes Trismegistus, Hermeticism, Table of Contents, Greek Manuscript, Renaissance Libraries</keywords>

<term>Pneumatics: The study of mechanical systems using compressed air or vacuum. Hero of Alexandria (1st century AD) was a pioneer in this field, inventing early steam engines and automated fountains.</term>
<term>Angelus Vergetius: A celebrated 16th-century Cretan calligrapher who worked in France for King Francis I. His handwriting was so beautiful it served as the model for the "Grecs du roi," the standard Greek typeface for centuries.</term>
<term>Ocellus Lucanus: A Pythagorean philosopher whose surviving work argues that the universe is uncreated and eternal.</term>

# † Heron of Alexandria’s Pneumatics, Book One †

**T**he study of <term>pneumatics<note>From the Greek 'pneuma,' meaning breath, air, or spirit. This field of ancient science focused on the mechanical properties of air, water, and steam.</note></term> having been deemed worthy of earnest attention by ancient philosophers and engineers—some of whom explained the theory behind its force, while others explained it through <gloss>per</gloss>ceptible <gloss>act</gloss>ion—we ourselves also consider it necessary to arrange the teachings handed down by the ancients into an orderly system, and to add those things which we have <gloss>dis</gloss>covered. For in this way, those who later wish to <gloss>be</gloss> involved in these mathematical studies will be benefited. Following the <gloss>con</gloss>struction of water-clocks, which <gloss>we</gloss> have already recorded in four books, we are writing this present work as a continuation of that subject, as previously stated. For through the combination of air, fire, water, and earth, and through the <gloss>three or</gloss> four [elements] being <gloss>com</gloss>bined, various effects are produced, some of which...

<margin><image-desc>Red ink circular library stamp: "Royal Library" <note>original Latin: "BIBLIOTHECA REGIA"</note></image-desc></margin>

<summary>This page serves as the introduction to Heron of Alexandria's "Pneumatics," where he acknowledges previous scholars and outlines his goal to organize and expand upon the science of air and liquid mechanics.</summary>
<keywords>Heron of Alexandria, pneumatics, engineering, four elements, water-clocks, Greek science</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>folio 1 verso, continuation of the preface to Heron of Alexandria’s Pneumatica. The text transitions from the general utility of mechanics to the specific physical theories underlying the work.</meta>

...providing for the most necessary requirements of life, while others display a certain astounding wonder.

## † Concerning the Vacuum †

**B**efore discussing what follows, we must first treat the subject of the <term>vacuum</term> <note>original: "κενοῦ" (kenou). In ancient physics, the existence of a "void" or empty space was a major point of debate between different schools of philosophy.</note>. For some say there is absolutely no vacuum; others say that while no <unclear>continuous</unclear> vacuum exists according to nature, there is one scattered in small particles through air, water, and other bodies. It is to these latter thinkers that we should most closely agree. For from the phenomena and from things falling under our direct perception, it will be shown in the following pages that this is what occurs. 

For if vessels that seem empty to most people are not, as they suppose, actually empty, but are full of air—and air is a <gloss>body</gloss> <note>original: "σῶμα" (sōma). In this context, "body" means a physical substance or matter that occupies space.</note>, as is agreed upon by those who study natural philosophy—it is composed of small and fine-particled bodies, which are for the most part invisible to us. If, therefore, one pours water into a vessel that appears to be empty, as much volume of water as enters the vessel, a corresponding volume of air will be displaced. 

One might understand what is being said from the following experiment. For if one turns a vessel that seems to be empty upside down and [plunges it] into water... <note>Heron is describing a classic experiment to prove air is matter: an inverted cup pushed into water will not fill with water because the air trapped inside acts as a physical barrier.</note>

<summary>Heron introduces the physical theory of the vacuum, arguing against an absolute void while supporting the idea of microscopic spaces between particles of matter. He begins an experiment with an inverted vessel to prove that air is a physical substance.</summary>
<keywords>vacuum, air, physical properties, Heron of Alexandria, atomism, experimental proof, pneumatic theory</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>Continuing from folio 1 verso, where Heron began describing an experiment with an inverted vessel to prove that air is a physical substance (a "body") that occupies space.</meta>

...but when it is pressed down and held <gloss>or crushed</gloss> so as to keep it in place, [the air] does not allow the water to enter into it. And the reason for this is clear: it demonstrates that while air was thought to be empty, it is not possible for water to be poured in besides it, because the entire space within the vessel is already filled. If, then, one drills a hole in the bottom of the vessel, the water will indeed flow into it through the mouth, while the air will escape through the hole. 

Furthermore, regarding the hole in the bottom: if one lifts the vessel straight up out of the water, raising it, the space within the vessel remains clear of the liquid, even though it was not previously [empty]; one must assume that bodies of air remain inside. For wind <term>pneuma</term> <note>original: "πνεῦμα" (pneuma). In ancient Greek, this word can mean breath, spirit, or wind. Here, Heron provides a strictly mechanical definition.</note> is produced by movement. For wind is nothing other than moving air. 

If, therefore, after drilling the hole in the bottom of the vessel, we draw it into a pool of water, and the air is forced toward the hole, one will perceive the wind rushing out of the vessel. This is nothing other than the air being driven out by the water. One should not suppose, therefore, that among existing things there is any [continuous] vacuum, or that air exists unmixed <gloss>possibly unmixed</gloss> by itself. Instead, [the vacuum] is scattered through it, in small particles...

νζ <note>original: "57"; likely a quire or folio signature.</note>

**Vocabulary:** air, water, wind/breath, vessel, vacuum, wind produced by motion.

<summary>Heron uses the experiment of a submerged vessel with a hole in its base to prove that air is a physical body that can be displaced by water, defining "pneuma" simply as air in motion.</summary>
<keywords>pneumatics, air as matter, pneuma, vacuum theory, Hero of Alexandria, experimental physics</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>Continuing from the discussion on the nature of air as a physical body and the existence of scattered vacuums between its particles. Heron is now extending this atomic theory to explain the properties of solids and the mechanism of compression.</meta>

This is also evident in solid bodies. Unless one were to think that diamonds <gloss>likely: do not interact</gloss> <note>original: "μὴ κοινωνοῦντας" (mê koinonountas). The scribe likely miscopied "μετὰ κινουμένους" (moving after); the context of the argument requires a word meaning 'unaffected' or 'not participating' in the surrounding medium.</note> when seen under water. Nor can they be set on fire, nor can their base be cut through, in the same way that anvils and hammers can be. This property follows naturally from their continuous density <term>pyknotēs</term> <note>original: "πύκνωτης" (pyknotēs). Heron uses this to describe how closely packed the atoms of a substance are.</note>. For the particles of fire, being coarser than the voids <term>kena</term> <note>original: "κενῶν" (kenon). Voids or empty spaces between particles.</note> within the stone, do not enter it, but instead remain only upon the outer surface. Consequently, they do not require fire to be drawn inside as happens with other bodies, nor do they receive heat <note>Heron is arguing that because the "atoms" of fire are larger than the tiny gaps in a diamond, the fire cannot penetrate the solid to heat or burn it.</note>.

The particles of air flow together toward one another, yet they do not fit together perfectly in every part. Instead, they have certain empty intervals <term>diastēmata</term> between them, just like sand in a vessel. One must assume that the grains of sand are like the particles of air, and the air between the grains of sand is like the voids between the particles of air. Therefore, when the air is compressed by some external force, it happens to settle into the spaces of the voids, because the bodies are pressed against one another. When the pressure is released, however, it returns again to the same arrangement... 

<summary>Heron explains that solids like diamonds are indestructible because their internal voids are too small for fire or tools to penetrate. He uses a famous analogy of sand in a jar to illustrate how air contains empty spaces, allowing it to be compressed and then spring back.</summary>

<keywords>atomism, vacuum, adamant, diamond, fire particles, density, compression of air, sand analogy, Heron of Alexandria, pneumatics</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>The text continues the argument that air consists of discrete particles separated by tiny voids, moving now from theoretical models to observable physical phenomena like elasticity and suction.</meta>

...to settle back into place, due to the **elasticity** <term>eutonia</term> <note>original: "εὐτονίᾳ" (eutonia). In this context, it refers to the inherent resilience or "tension" of a material that allows it to return to its original shape.</note> of these bodies. This is exactly what happens with shavings of horn and with **sponges**; for when they are compressed and then released, they settle back again into the same position and restore their original **volume** <term>onkos</term> <note>original: "ὄγκον" (onkos). This refers to the physical bulk or space occupied by the object.</note>. Similarly, if the particles of air are forced apart from one another by some external force and a larger **empty space** is created, they will run back toward each other again. For motion through a **vacuum** <term>kenon</term> <note>original: "κενοῦ" (kenou). Heron posits that particles move rapidly through empty spaces because there is no resistance.</note> happens very quickly, as there is nothing to resist or push back against them, until the particles eventually strike one another. 

If, therefore, someone takes a very light, narrow-mouthed vessel and applies it to their mouth, sucks out the air and then lets go, the vessel will remain hanging from the lips. This happens because the vacuum draws the flesh into itself so that the space emptied by the suction may be refilled. From this, it becomes clear that a collective vacuum was created within the space of the vessel. 

This is also evident from the **"medical eggs"** <note>original: "ἰατρικὰ ὠὰ" (iatrika oa). These were egg-shaped glass vessels used for cupping in ancient medicine.</note> which people apply to their mouths. When they wish to fill these vessels with liquid, they suck out the air inside them with their mouth, block the opening with a finger, and then turn the vessel upside down into the liquid. And the liquid is then drawn up...

<sig>57</sig>
<vocab>elasticity (eutonia), sponges (spongois), volume (onkon), force (bias), motion (phoran), medical eggs (iatrika oa), emptied (kenōthen)</vocab>

<summary>Heron explains the elasticity of air and solids using the analogy of sponges and horn shavings, concluding with a practical demonstration of vacuum pressure using cupping glasses and suction.</summary>
<keywords>elasticity, vacuum, suction, Heron of Alexandria, cupping glasses, physics of air, atomic theory</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues Heron of Alexandria’s Pneumatics. Having previously discussed the movement of air and the creation of a vacuum using one's mouth or "medical eggs," the author now applies these physical principles to the function of cupping glasses and the transformative nature of fire.</meta>

...[when] the finger [is removed], the liquid is drawn up into the emptied space under the water, and it is carried along by the upward motion occurring in the liquid. 

The phenomena regarding **cupping glasses** <term>sikyas</term> <note>original: "σικυῶν" (sikyon). These were gourd-shaped medical instruments used for bloodletting or drawing out excess fluids by creating a vacuum.</note> also occur in a way that is not foreign to the principles stated previously. For when these are applied to the body, not only do they not fall off—despite having significant weight—but they also draw the adjacent matter through the **pores** <term>araiomatōn</term> <note>original: "ἀραιωμάτων" (araiomatōn). This refers to the microscopic gaps or "thinning" in the structure of the flesh/skin through which fluids can be drawn.</note> of the body, for the following reason. 

When **fire** <term>pyr</term> <note>original: "πῦρ" (pyr).</note> is placed within the cupping glasses, it consumes and thins out the air contained inside them. Just as fire consumes other bodies, it transforms them into finer substances—namely water, air, and earth. That fire consumes matter is clear from the presence of particles of charcoal. For while the coals tend to maintain the same **volume** <term>onkos</term> <note>original: "ὄγκον" (onkos). This refers to the physical bulk or space occupied by the object.</note> they had from the beginning before undergoing **combustion** <term>kausin</term> <note>original: "καῦσιν" (kausin)</note>, or appear only slightly smaller, they differ greatly in weight from their original state. 

Fire separates the consumed bodies by means of **smoke** <term>kapnou</term> <note>original: "καπνοῦ" (kapnou).</note> into fiery, airy, and earthy substances. For the finer products of this consumption travel to the highest place, where the fire is located; but those slightly thicker than these...

<summary>This page explains the physics of medical cupping glasses, arguing that fire creates a vacuum by "thinning" the air inside the vessel, which then draws flesh and fluids into the space. It also touches on the ancient theory of the four elements and how combustion reduces the weight of matter while maintaining its volume.</summary>
<keywords>cupping glasses, vacuum, fire, combustion, four elements, Heron of Alexandria, suction, pores</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the transformative nature of fire and the behavior of particles. It moves from the consumption of solids (like charcoal) to the evaporation of liquids and the scientific causes of natural phenomena like dew and geothermal heat.</meta>

[finer] particles into the air. But those parts even thicker than these, 
having been carried upward to a certain degree <gloss>or to some extent</gloss> because of the 
continuity of the region, return again to the lower place to 
join with the earthy elements. Nor is it the case that water does not change into air 
when it is consumed by fire. For the **vapors** <term>atmoi</term> <note>original: "ἀτμοί" (atmoi). Heron uses this to describe steam or gaseous discharge from heated liquids.</note> from underlying 
cauldrons are nothing other than the particles of the liquid, being thinned, 
passing into the air. That fire dissolves 
all things thicker than itself and transforms them is 
clear from this. And from the **exhalations** <term>anathymiaseōn</term> <note>original: "ἀναθυμιάσεων" (anathymiaseōn). A classical scientific term for vapors rising from the earth or sea, often thought to produce wind, rain, or minerals.</note> occurring from the earth, 
it transforms certain thicker bodies 
into finer substances. For **dews** <term>drosoi</term> <note>original: "δρόσοι" (drosoi).</note> differ in no other 
way than by the water in the earth being thinned by 
some exhalation. This exhalation is produced by a certain 
fiery substance while the **sun** <term>hēlios</term> <note>original: "ἥλιος" (hēlios).</note> is beneath the earth, warming 
that region—especially when the soil is **sulfurous** <term>theiōdē</term> <note>original: "θειώδη" (theiōdē). Related to sulfur, which was known for its combustible and heat-producing properties.</note> or **bituminous** <term>asphaltōdē</term> <note>original: "ἀσφαλτώδη" (asphaltōdē). Referring to bitumen, a naturally occurring petroleum substance.</note>; 
being warmed, it creates an abundance of 
exhalations. And the **hot springs** <term>therma tōn hydatōn</term> <note>original: "θερμὰ τῶν ὑδάτων" (literally "the hot [parts] of the waters").</note> 
found in the earth arise from this same cause. Thus, of 
these substances, the finer parts change into air, while the 
thicker parts, having been carried upward to a certain degree, through the same ex[halation]...

<sig>57</sig>
**Vocabulary:** vapors, exhalations, dews, sun, sulfurous, bituminous, hot springs

<summary>Heron explains that fire and subterranean heat transform water and earthy matter into air and vapors, accounting for the creation of steam, dew, and hot springs. He attributes geothermal activity to the sun's position beneath the earth and the presence of combustible materials like sulfur.</summary>
<keywords>evaporation, exhalations (anathymiaseis), geothermal heat, dew, hot springs, Heron of Alexandria, physics of fire</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues Hero of Alexandria’s exploration of elemental transformation, moving from the creation of vapors and dews to the mechanical causes of wind and the physics of projectile motion. He concludes with the observation of water transitioning into earthy matter.</meta>

having been vaporized by force, as this [exhalation] cools down toward the <unclear>upper region</unclear> <note>original: "ὄλκιον" (olkion). The meaning is uncertain, but it likely refers to a vessel or a specific atmospheric layer where moisture collects.</note>, after a certain time it is carried again back to the lower place. And **winds** <term>pneumata</term> <note>original: "πνεύματα" (pneumata). While often translated as "spirits" or "breath," in Hero’s physics, it specifically refers to air in motion—wind.</note> also occur from a violent **exhalation** <term>anathymiaseōs</term> of the **air** <term>aeros</term>, as it is pushed out and thinned, always moving the parts of the air that follow in a continuous stream. But this movement of the air does not occur in all **places** <unclear>topois</unclear> equally. Instead, it is more violent near the exhalation itself, and weaker further away because of the <unclear>distance</unclear> <note>original: "δέπον" (depon). This may be a scribal error for "deon" (lack/distance) or "diastēma."</note> where it has not been moved. This is exactly what happens in the case of **heavy weights** <term>barōn</term> thrown upward. For indeed, these move quickly as long as they are near the <unclear>source</unclear> that sends them up, where the **force** <term>dynamis</term> that dispatched them is still active. But they move slowly as they reach the top [of their flight]. When the dispatched force no longer follows them at all, they are carried again to their natural place—I mean, of course, to the lower place. Just as the speed that the dispatching force imparted to them eventually ceased, so too, as the force gradually fails and is, as it were, spent, the speed of the motion also ends. And **water** <term>hydōr</term> also changes into an **earthy substance** <term>geōdē ousian</term>. For when we pour water into some earthy and hollow place, after not much time it disappears <note>Hero is describing what we now understand as absorption or evaporation, but he interprets it as a literal transformation of the liquid element into the solid element of earth.</note>.

<summary>Hero explains that wind is caused by the violent movement of air following an exhalation and uses the physics of thrown objects to illustrate how force is gradually exhausted. He also posits that water transforms into earth when it is absorbed by the ground.</summary>
<keywords>wind, aerodynamics, force (dynamis), projectile motion, elemental transformation, air, water, earth</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues Hero of Alexandria’s discussion on the transformation of elements. Having previously touched on winds and the movement of heavy bodies, he now addresses the physical evidence for water turning into earth and fire turning into air.</meta>

water is drunk up by earthy substance, so that it is mixed and itself becomes earth. If someone were to say that it is not shaped into or drunk up by the earth, but rather its moisture is drawn out and evaporated by heat—either from the sun or from something else—this should be shown to be false. For if water itself is put into some vessel made of <unclear>stone</unclear> <gloss>or bronze</gloss> or <unclear>some other</unclear> dense material, and placed in the sun for a long time, it is impossible for anyone to find that it has <unclear>decreased</unclear> by anything except the very smallest amount. Therefore, water changes into earthy substance. Indeed, silts and muds are all transformations of water into earth.

It also changes from a thinner substance into a thicker one, just as we see the flame of lamps being extinguished when it <unclear>lacks</unclear> oil. For as long as it is carried upward, as if hastening to reach its own place—I mean, of course, the **ether** <term>aithera</term> <note>original: "αἰθέρα" (aithera). In ancient physics, ether was the "fifth element," a divine, rarefied substance filling the upper universe above the terrestrial air.</note> which is above the air—but being overcome by the vast amount of air in between, it is no longer carried in a continuous stream. Instead, as if it were <note>original: "κερκαθεῖσα" (kerkatheisa). This likely means "shredded" or "broken into pieces," though the Greek term is rare or perhaps a scribal variation.</note> broken apart and intertwined with the particles of the air, it itself becomes air. The same thing is conceived regarding the air: that its types indeed...

<summary>Hero argues that elements can transform into one another, providing evidence that water turns into earth (as seen in mud) and flame dissolves into air when it loses its source of fuel.</summary>
<keywords>elemental transformation, water to earth, flame to air, ether, ancient physics, Hero of Alexandria</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to Hero of Alexandria’s *Pneumatics*. Following the previous page’s discussion on the transformation of elements (water into earth and fire into air), Hero now shifts focus to the existence of a "void" or vacuum (<term>kenon</term>). He uses the behavior of air and water in vessels to demonstrate how substances displace one another and how heat affects the density of matter.</meta>

<margin>8</margin>
It is necessary to take a vessel of modest size—as has indeed been observed—and if it is placed into water <unclear>in the vessel</unclear> at the opening of the vessel, and if we hold it with the opening facing upward, and the water falls into it, the air will depart from the vessel, being forced out by the mass of the water. Again, it is mixed and reshaped so that it becomes water. In this same way, as none of the air's substances are <unclear>dispersed</unclear> but are instead thinned by fire and escape through the pores <note>original: "ἀραιωμάτων" (araiomaton). This refers to the microscopic gaps or "interstices" between the particles of a solid material, such as the metal of a kettle.</note> of the cauldron [lebētos], the space within becomes empty—this applies to all surrounding matter, whatever kind may be employed. When it <unclear>departs</unclear> from its own place, the air rushes into that easily-moved space, for matter seeks to fill any displacement.

One must therefore say that while a universal **vacuum** <term>kenon</term> <note>original: "κενὸν" (kenon). Hero is navigating the ancient debate between the "Plenists" (who believed nature is full) and the "Atomists" (who believed in a void).</note> is indeed a void, it acts as an obstacle to the discovery of many things. Such a concept does not seem persuasive to reason when no divine proof for it is present. If, however, some phenomenon falling under our **sensory perception** <term>aisthēsin</term> <note>original: "αἴσθησιν" (aisthēsin). Hero emphasizes empirical evidence—what we can see and feel—over purely abstract philosophy.</note> can be shown—namely, that a vacuum exists in a collected state, whether occurring contrary to nature or according to nature, and that it is either fine-grained or compressed—it is also true that it fills the greatest part of the surrounding...

<meta>This page continues the technical demonstration from Hero of Alexandria’s *Pneumatics*. This is evident when we pour **water** <term>hydōr</term> <note>original: "ὕδωρ" (hydōr).</note> into any **vessel** <term>angeion</term> <note>original: "ἀγγεῖον" (angeion).</note>; the water drives out the **air** <term>aēr</term>, provided that the air previously contained within it can move out of the way. Thus, in no way could anything from the outside be introduced into the **sphere** <term>sphairan</term> unless some portion of the air already inside made way for it—at least, if the space were as dense and continuous as it is possible to be.

<meta>folio 8 recto. This page continues Hero of Alexandria’s discussion on the physics of fluids. Having previously explained the "scattered vacuum" between particles, he now addresses the paradox of hydrostatic pressure—specifically, why the weight of the ocean does not crush a diver. He invokes the authority of Archimedes to explain that within a body of water, water itself is "weightless" relative to the surrounding liquid.</meta>

[It] is equal in weight to itself. And this is not just an empty claim, for those **divers** <term>kolumbōntes</term> <note>original: "κολυμβῶντες" (kolumbōntes). Hero refers to sponge divers or salvage divers common in the ancient Mediterranean.</note> who go deep down are not crushed by the weight of the water above them. It must be demonstrated in the following way.

Let us imagine that within a vessel, starting from the surface of the object being pressed upon (that is, the surface where the water rests against it), there is a certain body of equal weight <term>isobares</term> <note>original: "ἰσοβαρὲς" (isobares). This describes an object with the same density as the fluid it is in, so it neither sinks nor floats.</note> within the liquid; for the liquids above possess this very quality. Suppose this body is placed into the liquid so that its lower surface fits perfectly against the object being pressed, as if it were the liquid itself, and that it rests there together with the rest of the liquid lying above it.

It is clear, then, that this body neither protrudes significantly above the rest of the liquid, nor will it sink beneath the surface of the liquid above it. For it was demonstrated by **Archimedes** <note>original: "Ἀρχιμήδει" (Archimēdei). The famous mathematician from Syracuse (c. 287–212 BCE) whose work laid the foundations for hydrostatics.</note> in his work ***On Floating Bodies*** <note>original: "ὀχουμένοις" (ochoumenois). Referring to Archimedes’ treatise *De iis quae in humido vehuntur*, where he defines the laws of buoyancy.</note> that bodies equal in weight to a liquid, when released into that liquid, will neither protrude above it nor sink to the bottom. Thus, it does not exert pressure on the things beneath it. 

Even if the <unclear>force</unclear> of the pressing things were removed, the body would remain in the same place. How then can a body exert pressure if it has no downward momentum <term>rhopēn</term> <note>original: "ῥοπὴν" (rhopēn). This refers to the "inclination" or weight that causes a scale to tip or an object to fall.</note>? On the contrary, such a liquid, acting as the same body, will not press upon the things lying beneath it <unclear>due to both its remaining at rest and its motion</unclear>.

<summary>Hero explains that water does not crush divers because water within water is "weightless" (isobaric), citing Archimedes' principle of buoyancy to prove that such bodies do not exert downward pressure.</summary>
<keywords>hydrostatics, Archimedes, buoyancy, divers, pressure, equilibrium, Hero of Alexandria</keywords>

<meta>folio 8 verso. Continuing from the discussion of Archimedes and hydrostatic pressure, Hero now transitions into his primary argument for the existence of a "scattered vacuum" (voids between particles). He uses the behavior of light, heat, and the mixing of liquids as empirical evidence that matter is not a solid block but contains microscopic gaps.</meta>

This aforementioned body differs from the surrounding liquid itself, but the fact that there are voids <term>kena</term> <note>original: "κενὰ" (kena). Hero uses this term to describe the microscopic "empty spaces" or "pores" between the particles of a substance.</note> can be understood from the following.

Nothing of such a nature—whether through water, or through air, or through any other body—could allow light to pass through it, nor could **heat** <term>thermotēs</term> <note>original: "θερμότης" (thermotēs).</note> or any other bodily power. For instance, consider how the **sun's rays** <term>hēliou aktinas</term> <note>original: "ἡλίου ἀκτῖνας" (hēliou aktinas).</note> pass through water to the bottom of a **vessel** <term>angeiou</term> <note>original: "ἀγγείου" (angeiou). A general term for a container, jar, or flask.</note>.

If it were the case that the water had no pores, but the light forced its way through the liquid by violence, it would follow that vessels already full would overflow. However, this is clearly not seen to happen. But this is also evident for another reason: if the light passed through the water by force, it would be deflected <note>original: "διαθλαθῆναι" (diathlathēnai). While this later became the technical term for "refraction," Hero uses it here in the sense of being broken or diverted by impact.</note> many times; it would not fail to be reflected <note>original: "ἀνακλώμεναι" (anaklōmenai). In ancient optics, this term covered both reflection and refraction.</note> toward the upper region, yet we see the rays reaching the bottom.

In reality, those rays which strike the actual particles of the water are, as it were, struck back and reflected toward the upper region. However, those rays which fall into the voids of the water, striking only a few particles, are the ones that reach the floor of the vessel. It is also clear that voids exist in the water from the fact that when wine is poured into water, it is seen to spread through all the [spaces].

<summary>Hero argues for the existence of microscopic voids within liquids by observing that light and heat pass through water without causing it to overflow. He explains that light is either reflected by water particles or passes through these "pores" to reach the bottom.</summary>
<keywords>vacuum, pores, light, heat, reflection, refraction, hydrostatics, Hero of Alexandria, atomism</keywords>

<meta>Folio 9 recto. This page continues Hero's argument regarding the "scattered vacuum." On the previous page, he began explaining that if water were a solid, continuous mass without internal pores, the entry of light or heat would displace the liquid and cause an overflow. Here, he concludes that thought and expands his theory to all physical matter.</meta>

...passing through the water. This would not happen if there were not **voids** <term>kenōn</term> <note>original: "κενῶν" (kenōn). These are the "pores" or microscopic gaps within the substance.</note> in the water. It is also evident that light passes through one thing because of the other. For when someone [kindles the flames of war] <note>original: "πολέμου ἀναφλοχνούς". This phrase is likely a corruption in the transcription; in similar texts by Hero, he refers here to the kindling of a fire or the rising of the sun to explain how light fills a space.</note>, it illuminates every measure, just as those same rays are carried again through the air. 

Furthermore, [heat] also passes through bronze and iron and all other **bodies** <term>sōmatōn</term> <note>original: "σωμάτων" (sōmatōn). Here meaning physical matter or solid objects.</note>. This is exactly what happens even at the [bottom of the sea] <note>original: "ὑφάλης της θαλασσίας". Likely referring to the seabed or deep-sea reefs being visible or reachable by light/heat.</note>. That a **collective void** <term>athroisma kenon</term> <note>original: "ἄθροισμα κενὸν" (athroisma kenon). Hero distinguishes between "scattered" voids (pores) and a "collective" or "continuous" vacuum where a large space is empty of matter.</note> can also occur according to nature is demonstrated through the [instrument] <note>original: "φωναρχείου" (phōnarcheiou). The meaning is unclear; it may refer to a bellows, a musical organ component, or a specific pneumatic device used in his experiments.</note> applied to the body, and through the use of a siphon, as shown in [diagram] D. 

Concerning the **nature of the void**, and though many more proofs could be offered, we consider those already mentioned to be sufficient. For through these we have established our proofs as true. 

Therefore, it remains to say for all things that every **body** consists of small-particled bodies, between which are scattered **voids** smaller than the particles themselves. For this reason, we say that no vacuum exists by [necessity] <note>Hero is articulating the famous "horror vacui" principle—that nature will not allow a large empty space to exist under normal conditions.</note>, provided no external force intervenes; rather, all things are full either of air or liquid or some other substance. In as much as one of these substances might depart, another follows immediately after it, filling up the place of that which is being emptied. Thus, there is absolutely no collective void [under normal circumstances].

<summary>Hero concludes his argument that matter is composed of tiny particles with microscopic "scattered voids" between them, explaining why light and heat can penetrate solids. He also introduces the principle that a large, continuous vacuum does not exist in nature unless forced, as matter will always rush in to fill an empty space.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical description of siphon mechanics from Folio 10, specifically addressing the equilibrium of forces and debunking a common scientific misconception regarding why water moves through the tube.</meta>

in the $\kappa$ part, the liquid weight $\beta \kappa$ again prevails and pulls <note>The author is describing how the weight of the water in the outer arm of the siphon exerts a downward force that "pulls" the rest of the liquid column.</note>. However, it flows only as long as the $\kappa$ mouth remains on the same level as the $\zeta \eta$ surface of the water. Then, for the same reason, it will no longer flow. But if the outer mouth of the siphon is lower than point $\alpha$, such as at point $\lambda$, it will continue to flow until the surface of the water reaches the level of the $\alpha$ mouth. Therefore, if we wish to empty all the water in the vessel, we should lower the siphon so that the $\alpha$ mouth touches the bottom of the vessel, leaving only enough space for the <unclear>water</unclear> to flow through.

Some people have offered the aforementioned explanation for the siphon, claiming that the larger leg <term>skelos</term> <note>original: "σκέλος" (skelos). The "legs" or branches of the siphon tube.</note>, because it contains more weight, pulls the smaller one. While this is a common belief, such a reason is a **false cause** <term>pseudes aitia</term> <note>original: "ψευδὴς αἰτία" (pseudes aitia). A logical fallacy where the wrong factor—in this case, the volume/weight of water rather than the pressure head—is identified as the cause of the motion.</note>. Anyone who believes this is greatly mistaken, as they might try to lower the water that has been drawn up using this logic.

We shall demonstrate this as follows: imagine a siphon where the inner leg is longer and narrow, while the outer leg is much wider but much shorter in length, so that this shorter, wider leg actually contains more water <note>By making the shorter arm wider, the author creates a scenario where the "shorter" side is heavier, testing the theory that weight alone determines the direction of flow.</note>. Let it be filled with water in this way. The larger leg of it

<meta>This page continues the technical critique of siphon mechanics, moving from a refutation of the "weight" theory to an explanation based on the equilibrium of liquid surfaces relative to the Earth's center.</meta>

Let it be placed into a vessel of water, or even into a well. According to [their] reasoning, because the outer leg <term>skelos</term> <note>original: "σκέλος" (skelos). A "leg" or branch of the siphon tube.</note> contains more water than the inner one—and especially when the outer leg is larger—it would then follow even in the well that, once it has begun to flow, the water would return of its own accord, since the liquid in the outer leg is greater than that in the inner leg.

But this is not seen to happen. Therefore, the reason they have given is not the true cause. Let us now examine the **natural cause**.

Since every continuous liquid at rest takes on a **spherical surface** <term>sphairikēn epiphaneian</term> <note>original: "σφαιρικὴν ἐπιφάνειαν" (sphairikēn epiphaneian). This refers to the ancient scientific principle—found in works like Archimedes' *On Floating Bodies*—that the surface of any body of water at rest is part of a sphere having the same center as the Earth.</note>, extending toward the center of the Earth. When it is not at rest, it flows. If, therefore, as has been said, the liquid reaches a single spherical surface, [it will rest]. 

Suppose then we take two vessels and pour liquid into each. Then, after filling the siphon <note>original: "σίφωνα" (siphona)</note> and closing its mouths with our fingers, let us place one leg of it into one of the vessels, dipping it into the water, and the remaining leg into the other vessel. The whole of the water will become continuous, for the liquid in each of the vessels joins with the liquid in the siphon. Consequently, all the water becomes continuous once again...

<summary>The author refutes the theory that siphons work simply because one side is heavier, arguing instead that liquids naturally seek a state of equilibrium based on a "spherical surface" centered on the Earth.</summary>
<keywords>hydrostatics, siphon, spherical surface, liquid equilibrium, physics experiment, continuity of water</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page concludes the technical argument regarding how liquid moves through a siphon. It moves from the previous page's discussion of "continuous liquid" to explain why a siphon stops flowing once the water levels in two vessels are equal.</meta>

In those vessels where the surface comes to be on a single plane <term>epiphaneia</term> <note>original: "ἐπιφάνεια" (epiphaneia). In ancient geometry, this refers to the "face" or level of the liquid.</note>, the liquid in each of them will come to rest <term>ēremēsei</term> <note>original: "ἠρεμήσει" (ēremēsei). To be still or in a state of equilibrium.</note> once the siphon has been inserted. Because the water has become continuous, it is necessary for all of it to flow toward the lower part due to this continuity, until all the water in the vessels reaches a single surface, or until one of the vessels is emptied. 

Suppose, then, that the liquids in the vessels have reached a single surface as described. It will, therefore, be at rest. For the liquid within the siphon will come to rest along with them. If we were to imagine the siphon being "cut off" <note>This is a thought experiment where the author mentally separates the part of the tube above the water line from the part below.</note> at the level of the surfaces in the vessels, even then the liquid within the siphon would remain at rest. 

And even if the siphon is lifted upward and is not tilted toward either side, the liquid will again remain at rest. This holds true whether the width of the tube is equal throughout, or if one leg <term>skelos</term> <note>original: "σκέλος" (skelos).</note> is much larger than the other. The liquid's state of rest did not occur because of the size of the legs, but rather because its openings <note>original: "στόμια" (stomia), literally 'mouths'.</note> were positioned in equilibrium <term>zygon</term> <note>original: "ζυγόν" (zygon). Literally a "yoke" or "balance scale"; here meaning the openings are at the same horizontal level.</note>. 

How is it, then, that when the siphon is raised, the liquid is carried downward toward whichever weight lies beneath it [having] lighter air? Because indeed... <unclear>mass/total</unclear>...

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of siphon mechanics, shifting from the equilibrium of liquids to the specific role of air pressure and the process of "priming" a siphon by mouth to move liquid upward against its natural gravity.</meta>

<page-num>72</page-num>

Of that which is being <unclear>drawn up</unclear>, every outer space is brought down by replacement <note>The Greek term here suggests the concept of *antiperistasis*, where one substance moves to fill the space vacated by another.</note>. First, the upper part of the siphon <term>siphōn</term> <note>original: "σίφων" (siphōn). A tube used to move liquid from a higher to a lower container.</note> must be filled; once air has entered this space, it has no power to act anywhere. If, therefore, someone were to pierce the upper part of the siphon, the liquid will immediately break out and flow down, as the air finds other paths through the opening. Before anything happens to the liquid resting in the siphon due to the underlying air <term>aēr</term> <note>original: "ἀήρ" (aēr). In this context, it refers to the atmospheric pressure or the volume of air inside the tube.</note>, the water <term>hydōr</term> <note>original: "ὕδωρ" (hydōr).</note> is squeezed out; having nowhere else to go, it no longer admits the liquid. But when, through the act of breathing in, the obstructing air <note>original: "πονηρὸς ἀὴρ" (poneros aer). Literally "bad air," here referring to the air that prevents the vacuum from forming.</note> moves to where it can go, the weight of the water, no longer being held up, departs. For the same reason, the things in the siphon are drawn upward against nature <note>original: "παρὰ φύσιν" (para physin). In ancient physics, water "naturally" moves down. Moving up is considered "against nature" and requires a vacuum or external force.</note> by the mouth. For by receiving the air from within the siphon into ourselves, we become more full than we were before. If we have this air within us, the process continues from the beginning until the emptying <term>kenōsis</term> <note>original: "κένωσις" (kenōsis). The act of creating a vacuum or clearing the tube.</note> occurs at the surface <term>epiphaneia</term> <note>original: "ἐπιφάνεια" (epiphaneia). The top level or "face" of the liquid.</note> of the wine <term>oinos</term> <note>original: "οἶνος" (oinos).</note>. And then the wine, being compressed <term>thlibomenos</term> <note>original: "θλιβόμενος" (thlibomenos). Pushed or squeezed by atmospheric pressure.</note>, will move into the space of the siphon as it is being emptied. For at the same moment, the space becomes empty where the liquid, being pushed, will travel. For this reason, its motion <note>original: "φορά" (phora). Movement or carrying force.</note> toward the upper part occurs even though it is against its natural tendency.

<meta>Folio 72 verso. This page continues a geometric and physical proof regarding the equilibrium of liquids within a siphon, grounded in the ancient Greek understanding of the Earth as the center of the cosmos.</meta>

And the liquid itself will come to rest within the siphon when the <term>spherical surface</term> <note>original: "σφαιρικὴ ἐπιφάνεια" (sphairikē epiphaneia). In ancient physics, particularly in the works of Archimedes, the surface of any liquid at rest is understood to be a part of a sphere whose center is the same as the center of the Earth.</note> is at the same distance from the earth. Since the surface of any wine <note>original: "οἴνου" (oinou). Though water was discussed previously, the author here uses wine as the specific liquid, likely referring to the contents of a mechanical wine-dispensing vessel.</note> is naturally spherical, it comes to rest when it shares the same <term>center as the earth</term> <note>original: "μέσον... τῆς γῆς" (meson tēs gēs).</note>. For if it were not at rest, it would remain in motion until it reached such a state of rest. Therefore, let its spherical surface be at rest, sharing the center of the earth.

And let it intersect the previous surface. For as the same liquid is drawn up by a certain vacuum, they cause <unclear>all</unclear> of it to be held up from a single point. Let both surfaces, therefore, be cut through the center of the earth by a certain plane. And let them create lines on the surface of the circumference of a circle, sharing the same center as the earth. And let them designate the points **a, b, g, d, e, z, i** <note>These represent the Greek letters alpha, beta, gamma, delta, epsilon, zeta, and eta used as labels for a geometric diagram.</note>. And I will show that **b-i** is equal to **i-theta**, and each is equal to **i-z**. This is absurd <note>original: "ἄτοπον" (atopon). A technical term in Greek mathematics meaning "impossible" or "contradictory," used here to conclude a proof by showing that the alternative leads to a logical impossibility.</note>. Therefore, it will come to rest.

<meta>Folio 74 recto. Introduction of a new problem: seeking uniform flow. Continues the discussion of the "pneumatic siphon" from the previous page, shifting focus from how it works to how to fix the problem of varying flow rates as the water level drops.</meta>

As has been stated, the pneumatic siphon **s-h-h-z**—or pneumatic compass <note>original: "διαβήτης" (diabētēs) — literally a compass or calipers, often used by ancient engineers to describe the arched shape of a siphon.</note>—[operates in this way]. From what has been demonstrated previously, it is clear that the flow produced through a siphon, when the air remains still, is irregular <note>original: "ἀνώμαλος" (anōmalos) — meaning uneven or non-uniform.</note>. The flow suffers from this irregularity because of the [changing level] of the liquid in the containing vessel as it flows toward the bottom. 

For in this case, the flow is indeed irregular: at the beginning, the discharge of water is pressed by the greater weight [of the water column above], but as the process reaches its end <note>original: "ἀκμάζουσα" (akmazousa) — literally "ripening" or "peaking," here referring to the final stages before the vessel is empty.</note>, it is pressed by less weight. And the greater the external extension <note>original: "ὑπεροχή" (hyperochē) — the length of the siphon arm outside the vessel.</note> of the siphon, the faster the flow becomes. Conversely, the outflow through its mouth is pressed by more weight when the external extension is shorter—that is, the distance between the surface of the water in the vessel and the external mouth of the siphon. 

If, therefore, it has been said that the flow through the siphon is always irregular, it is necessary to find a way for the flow through the siphon to be always uniform <note>original: "ὁμαλήν" (homalēn) — smooth, even, or constant.</note>.

***

Whenever we wish a second <term>flow: "ῥύσις" (rhysis) — the discharge or stream of liquid</term> to occur through it—faster than the one previously mentioned, yet uniform in itself—we turn the screw so that the bar **o-p** is brought lower.

And for this reason, the flow will be uniform in itself, but faster than the previous one. 

If we desire it still faster again, we turn the screw so that the bar **o-p** becomes even lower. If, however, we wish it to be slower, the bar **o-p** must be raised higher. And in this way, the flow of the siphon will be either faster or slower, while remaining uniform rather than irregular. In order that...

| **παραλληλεπίπεδον** | parallelepiped | A six-sided prism where every face is a parallelogram. |
| **διάφραγμα** | partition | A wall or membrane separating two chambers in a pneumatic device. |

<meta>This page continues the technical description of the fire engine's internal mechanics, specifically focusing on the valve assembly and the lever system that operates the pistons.</meta>

...let [the valves] be inserted, either glued or otherwise joined to the bottoms of the **cylinders**; let these have covers from above, so that the **valves** <term>valves: "τυμπάνια" (tympania) — literally "little drums," these act as circular check-valves that allow water to flow in only one direction</term> cannot escape from them. Let the **pistons** be attached to upright, integrated **rods** <term>rods: "κανόνια" (kanonia) — the vertical bars that transmit force from the handle to the pistons</term> at their centers, labeled **E, Z** <note>original: "ε ζ"</note>. To these rods, let a crossbar **P, R** <note>original: "π ρ"</note> be attached, which moves around its center on a fixed **pivot pin** <term>pivot pin: "περόνην" (peronen) — a central axle or bolt that allows the lever to rock back and forth</term> **M**. Let the rods **E, Z** also be connected to the crossbar by pins at **B, G** <note>original: "β γ"</note>.

To the horizontal pipe **X, O, E, Z** <note>original: "ξ ο η ζ"</note>, let another upright pipe be joined, **X, P** <note>original: "ξ π"</note>, which is split into a **fork** at **P**, and let it have **nozzles** <term>nozzles: "κρηνίσματα" (krenismata) — the discharge points or "little fountains" through which the water is projected</term>, through which it will force the water. This is similar to what was mentioned previously regarding the vessel that forces water back, because of the **air compressed** within it. 

If, therefore, the aforementioned cylinders along with their surrounding structure are placed into a water vessel **G, E, Th, M** <note>original: "γ η θ μ"</note>, and the crossbar **P, R** is pumped from its ends (such as at **P, A**), moving back and forth around the pivot **M**, the pistons, as they descend, will force the liquid out through the pipe **Kh, X** <note>original: "χ ξ"</note> and through the tube **M, N** <note>original: "μν"</note> at the joint. <summary>This page describes the mechanical assembly of a dual-cylinder pump, detailing the lever system (crossbar and rods) and the use of check-valves to create pressure. It highlights the use of compressed air to maintain a steady stream of water, a sophisticated feature of ancient engineering.</summary>
<keywords>piston rods, check-valves, pivot pin, compressed air, hydraulic mechanics, Hero of Alexandria, fire engine</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>Continuation of the technical description of the hydraulic machine, following the assembly of the pistons and the central lever mechanism.</meta>

...of the **valve C, Ps** <note>original: χ ψ</note>, it shuts the **intake valve** <term>intake valve: "ῥανιστάριον" (rhanistarion) — a small regulating valve or "sprinkler" component that controls fluid entry</term>. As it is lowered, the part **U** <note>original: υ</note> closes, while **R** <note>original: ῥ</note> opens. Through this, the water is forced upward and exerts counter-pressure. These same actions occur with the **piston K, L** <note>original: κλ</note>. 

An **adjustable pipe** <term>adjustable pipe: "σωληνάριον" (solenarion) — a small tube or nozzle extension</term> that tilts both upward and downward allows the **discharge** <term>discharge: "ἐκπιλισμόν" (ekpilismon) — the act of squeezing or forcing out the liquid under pressure</term> to be directed toward any desired height, rather than being tilted only at a fixed angle. By rotating on a **pivot pin**, the machine is kept from wobbling at the side-frame. This mechanism is slow and intended for land-based use, as is understood from the practical needs already mentioned.

To ensure the liquid is discharged more easily into the designated area, let us construct the **pipe X, P** <note>original: ξ π</note> from two sections joined along their length, which are **precision-fitted** <term>precision-fitted: "συνεσμηρισμένων" (synesmerismenon) — surfaces ground smooth (often with emery) to create a water-tight yet movable sliding seal</term> to one another. One of these sections should be permanently attached to the **pipe X, O, E, Z** <note>original: ξ ο η ζ</note>, while the other is attached to the **forked piece**. By rotating this toward the points **S** <note>original: ς</note> and by the tilting of the upper pipe at **M** <note>original: μ</note>, the pressurized water can be aimed toward any place we desire. This upper fitted pipe also features small covers to prevent it from being blown out of the machine by the force of the liquid. There are also **L-shaped brackets** <term>L-shaped brackets: "γαμμοειδῆ" (gammoeide) — brackets shaped like the Greek letter Gamma (Γ)</term> soldered to it, which rotate around a pin...

### By Means of Displacement

<image-desc>A complex mechanical diagram in red ink depicting a hydraulic automaton, possibly an artificial bird that "drinks." It shows three main rectangular containers. One leg of this siphon extends out through the bottom of the vessel. **I**n Egyptian temples, bronze <term>wheels</term> <note>original: τροχοὶ (trochoi)</note> are placed near the <term>doorposts</term> <note>original: παραστάσι (parastasi); the pillars or jambs flanking a temple entrance.</note> so that they may be rotated. Inside this tube is another small tube, **L M** <note>original: λ μ (lambda, mu)</note>. Let the part **L** be soldered to **Z E Th K**. At the mouth, there is also a hole, **P R** <note>original: π ρ (pi, rho)</note>.

### To construct a lamp that advances its own wick

Let the lamp be **Alpha Beta Gamma**. Through its mouth, let an iron <term>pin</term> <note>original: περόνη (peronē)</note> **Delta Epsilon** be passed, moving easily about the point **Epsilon**. Around this pin, the <term>wick</term> <note>original: ἐλλύχνιον (ellychnion)</note> must be wound so that it is flexible. Let there also be attached a toothed <term>drum</term> <note>original: τυμπάνιον (tympanion); a small gear or cogwheel.</note> **Zeta**, moving smoothly about a small axle. Its teeth should touch the pin, so that as the drum rotates, the wick is pushed forward by the teeth. 

The lamp should have an opening <margin><unclear>13</unclear></margin> at the <term>reservoir</term> <note>original: ὀμφαλὸν (omphalon); literally "navel," referring here to the central oil container.</note> that is somewhat larger. When the oil is poured in, let a <term>float</term> <note>original: κυβητείον (kybēteion); likely a block of cork or light wood.</note> **Eta** swim upon it, having an attached, upright <term>rod</term> <note>original: κανόνιον (kanonion)</note> **Theta**, which is toothed and meshed with the teeth of the drum. It follows then, as the <term>oil</term> <note>original: ἔλαιον (elaion)</note> is consumed, the float will descend...

<note>The mechanism described here is a clever application of a rack-and-pinion gear. As the oil level drops, the float falls, pulling the toothed rod (the rack) down. This turns the gear (the drum), which grips and feeds the wick forward into the flame.</note>

# A vessel is also constructed which, in appearance, receives the liquid you pour in; however, if you manage it differently, it will stop receiving it.

This is constructed as follows. Let there be a vessel **Alpha Beta**, with its neck partitioned by a <term>diaphragm</term> <note>original: διαφράγματι (diaphragmati); a horizontal dividing plate or partition.</note>. Through this diaphragm, let a tube **Epsilon Zeta** be let down, positioned slightly away from the bottom of the vessel, but extending above the diaphragm so as to stay a short distance from the lip of the vessel. 

Around this tube, let another tube **Eta Theta** be placed, leaving enough space from the diaphragm for water to flow through. At the top of tube **Epsilon Zeta**, the outer tube **Eta Theta** should be sealed off at its upper parts with a **small plate** <note>original: λεπιδίῳ (lepidiō); a thin metal disc or scale used as a cap.</note>. 

The vessel should also have a **double-pipe** <note>original: δίαυλον (diaulon); an air-passage or vent-pipe.</note> **Kappa Upsilon**, which leads into the **main body** <note>original: κύτος (kytos); the central reservoir or cavity of the vessel.</note>. 

Now, when we pour the liquid through the neck, it will happen that the liquid passes through tube **Eta Theta** and then through **Epsilon Zeta** into the main body, while the air within escapes through the **Kappa** air-passage. 

If, however, we pause and tilt the neck of the vessel, the air will interrupt the continuity of the flow. Consequently, the liquid remaining in the **Eta Theta** tube, rushing down, will fall onto the diaphragm. Let the width around the **Eta Theta** tube be of such a size that...

### The construction of a mechanism so that when a fire is lit, the doors open automatically, and when the fire is extinguished, they close again.

**C**onstruction <note>original: Κατασκευή (Kataskeue); refers to the technical design or assembly of the device.</note> to enable doors to open automatically when a fire is lit, and to close again once the fire is put out. Let there be a <term>circular shrine</term> <note>original: περιφερικός ναΐσκος (peripherikos naiskos); a small, decorative temple model.</note> upon a base marked **Alpha Beta**, upon which a <term>small altar</term> <note>original: βωμίσκος (bomiskos)</note> **Epsilon Zeta** is placed. Through this small altar, we shall pass a tube **Eta Zeta**, whose mouth **Zeta** should be inside the altar. The other end, **Eta**, is to be enclosed in a certain <term>sphere</term> <note>original: σφαῖρα (sphaira)</note> **Kappa**, placed a short distance from its center. The sphere must be sealed airtight to the tube **Eta Zeta**. Let there also be a <term>siphon</term> <note>original: σίφων (siphon)</note> **Kappa Lambda Mu** within the sphere. The <term>pivots</term> <note>original: στρόφιγγες (strophinges); the vertical axles of the doors.</note> of the doors should be extended down into the lower part of the device, and they should rotate upon <term>hinge-pins</term> <note>original: κνωδάξιν (knodaxin); the pivot points or sockets that allow the door axles to turn smoothly.</note>,

...to the small chains around the **pivots** <term>pivots (strophingas)</term> <note>original: στρόφιγγας; the vertical axles or hinges upon which the temple doors turn</note> just as was learned in the previous <unclear>demonstration</unclear>, with the weight **Lambda** hanging down. For once again, the **air** <term>air (aer)</term> within the small altar is heated and expands; it will pass through the tube **Zeta Eta** into the **small leather bag** <term>small leather bag (askomalion)</term> <note>original: ἀσκομάλιον; a small skin or bellows that acts as a pneumatic actuator</note>, and it will lift the bag together with the weight **Kappa**. Consequently, the doors will be opened automatically by this means. It is designed so that they might also close when the altar is heated, if the same action were to occur by means of an equivalent weight. However, when the fire is extinguished and the excess air within the small leather bag departs <note>as the air cools and contracts, creating a vacuum that pulls the air back</note>, the weight, moving downward along with the small leather bag, will pull and close the doors.

### On a vessel being full of wine, and having three taps, through which this wine flows in turn.

**A** vessel being full of **wine** <term>wine (oinos)</term> and having three **taps** <term>taps (krounoi)</term> <note>original: κρουνοὺς; spouts or nozzles for discharging liquid</note>, through which this wine flows in turn. But when we pour in **water** <term>water (hydor)</term>, the wine no longer flows, but the water itself flows through the remaining two taps. Then, when the water ceases to flow, the wine flows again in turn. This occurs as many times as we pour in water. Let there be a vessel, **Alpha Beta**, divided by the **partition** <term>partition (diaphragma)</term> **Epsilon Zeta**. Near the **base** <term>base (pythmen)</term>, let there be a tap...

### Construction of a Hydraulic Organ

Let there be a certain bronze <term>pedestal<note>original: "βωμίσκος" (bōmiskos); literally a "small altar," commonly used as a decorative housing for pneumatic machines.</note></term>, designated **α β γ η**, containing water. Within the water, let there be an inverted hollow <term>hemisphere<note>original: "ἡμισφαίριον" (hēmisphairion).</note></term> called a <term>chest<note>original: "πυξὶς" (pyxis); a box or container, here acting as a reservoir to hold compressed air.</note></term>, designated **α β γ δ**, which has openings at its base to allow water to flow through to the interior parts.

From the top of this chest, let two pipes extend upward, joined to it, reaching above the pedestal. One pipe is **η κ λ μ**, which is bent toward the outer part of the pedestal and connected to another <term>pump-cylinder<note>original: "πυξίδι" (pyxidi); Heron uses the same word "pyxis" for the air reservoir and the pump cylinder.</note></term> **ν ξ ο π**. This cylinder has its mouth facing downward, and its inner surface is machined perfectly straight to receive a <term>piston<note>original: "ἔμβολέα" (embolea); a plunger or piston used to force air into the machine.</note></term>.

To this cylinder, let there be a well-fitted piston **ρ ς**, so that no air can leak past it. Attached to the piston, let there be a very strong rod **τ υ**. To this, another rod **υ φ** is joined...

When a downward motion is produced through the point **φ**, the **piston** <term>ρ ϛ</term> <note>original: "ἐμβολεύς" (emboleus); the plunger or piston of the air pump.</note>, being raised, will squeeze out the air contained within the **cylinder** **ν ξ ο π**. This air will close the hole in the small cylinder **ω** by means of the previously mentioned **flap-valve** <note>original: "πλατυσμάτιον" (platysmation); a small disc acting as a one-way valve to prevent air from escaping back out the intake.</note>.

The air will then pass through the pipe **μ κ η** and into the **wind-regulator** <term>pnigeus</term> <note>original: "πνιγέα" (pnigea); literally the "choker," an inverted hemispherical vessel submerged in water that acted as a pressure reservoir to ensure a steady wind supply.</note>. From the wind-regulator, it will pass into the **transverse pipe** **4 π** via the pipe **ε ζ**. From this transverse pipe, the air will proceed into the **organ pipes** <note>original: "αὐλοὺς" (aulous).</note>, provided that the holes in the **sliders** are aligned vertically with the pipes.

That is to say, this occurs whenever the **sliders** <note>original: "πώματα" (pōmata); literally "lids" or "covers," these are the sliding keys that open or close the windway to the pipes.</note>—either all of them or some of them—are pushed in. Therefore, to ensure that when we intend one of the pipes to sound, the holes corresponding to them are opened, and when we wish them to cease, they are closed, we construct the following mechanism.

These components should be understood as being situated within the **slider-chest** <term>glōssokomon</term> <note>original: "γλωσσοκόμῳ" (glōssokomō); the box or "tongue-case" that houses the sliding mechanisms.</note>, excluding the pipe **μ κ**. Let the opening of the chest be **η**, and the pipe bored to match this be **ε**. Let there be a slider fitted to it, **π ζ**, having a hole **η** which is currently offset from the pipe **ε**. 

There must also be a certain three-limbed **crank-lever** <term>ankōniskos</term> <note>original: "ἀγκωνίσκος" (ankōniskos); a "little elbow" or bell-crank used to convert the player's touch into the horizontal sliding motion of the key.</note>, **λ ξ μ**, of which the limb **λ ξ** is attached to the slider **π ζ**.

Near the point **μ**, let the center of the lever **μ** move around a pin <note>original: "περόνην" (peronēn); a pivot or axle-pin around which the lever rotates.</note>. If, therefore, we press down with the hand the end **ξ** of the lever—which is the <term>finger-key</term> <note>original: "δακτυλίσκον" (daktyliskon); literally "little finger," referring here to the key or lever-end touched by the musician's finger.</note>—toward the opening **μ** of the valve-box, we will push the **slider** into the inner part. Thus, when it reaches the interior, the hole within it will be aligned vertically with the hole of the **organ pipe**. 

Now, in order that when we release our hand, the **slider** is automatically pulled back out and disconnects from the pipe, the following should be prepared. Let a rod be placed under the valve-boxes, equal in length to the **transverse pipe** **4 π**, and lying parallel to it at **μη μ**. In this rod, let there be fixed <term>horn-springs</term> <note>original: "σπαθία κεράτινα" (spathia keratina); literally "horn spatulas." These are strips of horn used as resilient springs, a common feature in ancient Greek mechanics.</note> that are well-tensioned and curved. One of these should be **μ ζ**, situated near the **μ σ** valve-box. 

From the tip of this horn-spring, let a <term>sinew-string</term> <note>original: "νεῦρα" (neura); strings made of animal sinew, used for their strength and slight elasticity.</note> be attached and tied to the end **ξ**. The arrangement is such that when the slider is pushed inward, the sinew-string is stretched taut. If, then, by holding down the end **μ** of the finger-key, we push the slider into the inner part, the sinew-string will pull on the horn-spring, so that its natural curve is forcibly straightened. When we release the key, the horn-spring, returning again to its original curved position, will pull back the **slider** of the finger-key.

...[contained] in the **air-chamber** <note>original: "πνιγεῖ" (pnigei); literally "the smotherer," Hero's term for the inverted bell-shaped chamber that sits in water to maintain air pressure.</note>, I mean the air pushed out from the **cylinder** <note>original: "πυξίδος" (pyxidos); the pump barrel or box.</note> whenever the water <unclear>demonstrates</unclear> that it is compressing the air, so that the **pipes** <note>original: "αὐλοὺς" (aulous)</note> are able to sound.

A pin must be pushed through this fitting. This is so the piston is not twisted or distorted, but can be lowered and raised perfectly straight.

...the force <unclear>of the drive</unclear> through its own action. Let there be attached to this a **rod** <term>M-N<note>original: "μ ν" (mu-nu); labels referring to a technical diagram.</note></term>, joined to another **rod** <term>N-X<note>original: "ν ξ" (nu-xi)</note></term>, moving around the **axle** <term>R-P<note>original: "ρ π" (rho-pi)</note></term> and let a well-fitting <unclear>N</unclear> **pin** be attached. At the point **X**, let a **small plate** <note>original: "πλατισμάτιον" (platysmation); a flat flange or lever-arm.</note> be attached and fused to it, labeled **X-O**. 

Alongside this **X-O** plate, let there be an **axle** <term>S<note>original: "σ" (sigma)</note></term>, rotating in the middle upon **iron pivots** <note>original: "κνώδακας σιδηροῦς" (knōdakas sidērous); these are the metal pins or gudgeons that allow the axle to spin with minimal friction.</note> in a frame that is capable of being moved. 

<margin>+</margin> and to **S** <gloss>H</gloss> let there be attached two **drums**, **T** and **Ph** <note>original: "τ φ" (tau-phi); these function as pulleys or cam-wheels.</note>. 

Of these, let **T** have **pegs** <note>original: "σκυτάλια" (skytalia); small cylindrical rods projecting from the wheel to act as cams.</note> resting upon the **X-O** plate. Let the other drum **Ph** have **vanes**, like the so-called **wind-vanes** <note>original: "ἀνεμούρια" (anemouria); Heron is referring to a windmill-like structure to catch the breeze.</note>. 

Therefore, whenever all the **vanes** are driven by the wind and they turn the **Ph** drum, the axle will also be turned. Consequently, the **T** drum and the pegs on it, striking the **X-O** plate at intervals, will raise the **piston** <term>K-L<note>original: "κ λ" (kappa-lambda)</note></term>. As the peg moves away, the piston will be brought down and will squeeze the air in the **cylinder** <term>H-Th<note>original: "η θ" (eta-theta)</note></term> into the **pipes** and **flutes**, and thus it will produce the sound. The frame that holds the axle must be of such a kind that it always turns toward the blowing wind.

<vocab>wind-vanes, piston, drums/cams, rod, axle</vocab>

<summary>This page describes one of the earliest known designs for a windmill-powered machine, using a wind-vane and cam-system to automate the air pump of an organ. It explains how wind energy is converted into mechanical motion to lift a piston and force air into the instrument's pipes.</summary>

### How the sounds of various small birds may be produced.

<margin>
oui
~
</margin>
Let there be a watertight **vessel** <note>original: "ἀγγεῖον" (angeion)</note> through which a **funnel** <note>original: "χώνη" (chōnē)</note> passes, the **stem** <note>original: "καυλὸς" (kaulos); the pipe or neck of the funnel</note> of which should stay far enough from the **bottom** <note>original: "πυθμένος" (pythmenos)</note> for water to flow through. It follows, therefore, since it is set upon a pivot, that it remains upright.

### How to make a sphere dance.

### Transparent spheres are also constructed, containing within themselves 
air and liquid. In the center of this is a small **sphere** <note>original: "σφαιρίον" (sphairion); the diminutive form suggests a small model</note> serving as a **model of the cosmos** <term>cosmos</term> <note>original: "κόσμου" (kosmou); referring to the ancient Greek conception of the universe as a series of concentric spheres.</note>. Two glass **hemispheres** <term>hemispheres</term> <note>original: "ἡμισφαίρια ὑάλινα" (hēmisphairia hyalina)</note> are fashioned. One of these is sealed with a **bronze plate** <note>original: "λεπίδι χαλκῇ" (lepidi chalkē); literally a bronze scale or thin sheet</note> that has a hole in its center. The small sphere representing the **Earth** <note>original: "γῆ" (gē)</note> is smaller and light. This small sphere is placed into water within the other hemisphere. Then, the partitioned hemisphere is fitted onto it. When the liquid is withdrawn from the water, the small sphere will be held in the middle position. Once the other hemisphere is joined around it, the proposed device is complete. +

### The device known as the **Dripper** from which the sun draws its effect:
<margin>
7
~
</margin>
Let there be a water-tight **base** <term>base</term> <note>original: "βάσις" (basis)</note> labeled **a b c d**. Through this, a **funnel** <note>original: "χώνη" (chōnē)</note> passes, the stem of which should be a very short distance from the bottom. Let there also be a **sphere** <note>original: "σφαῖρα" (sphaira)</note> at **e f**. From this, a **tube** <note>original: "σωλήν" (sōlēn)</note> is to be carried into the base, remaining a short distance from the bottom of the vessel and the wall of the small sphere. A curved **pivoted joint** <note>original: "στρόφιγξ" (strophingx); refers to a turning mechanism or valve</note> <unclear>having been fitted</unclear> into the small sphere, serves to close off the funnel.

<image-desc>
A mechanical diagram showing several components. In the upper left, two hemispherical bowls are shown, one inverted over the other, connected by a curved pipe. To the right is a vertical wheel with a small tube or axle through its center. Below these is a rectangular base labeled with Greek letters α, β, γ, δ. A tube extends from the upper assembly down into this base.
</image-desc>

And let water be poured into the small sphere. Therefore, when the **sun** <note>original: "ἥλιος" (hēlios)</note> shines upon the small sphere, the air within it, having been heated, will squeeze out the liquid. This liquid will be carried out through the **siphon** <term>siphon</term> <note>original: "σίφωνος" (siphōnos)</note> and will pass through the funnel into the base. <note>This mechanism relies on the fact that air expands when heated. As the sun warms the metal or glass sphere, the internal pressure increases, forcing the water out through the only available exit.</note>

**Dripper** <note>original: "λίβας" (libas)</note>, **Sun** <note>original: "ἥλιος" (hēlios)</note>, **Base** <note>original: "βάσις" (basis)</note>, **Siphon/Tube** <note>original: "σίφων" (siphōn)</note>, **To heat** <note>original: "θερμαίνω" (thermainō)</note>

...of the **rod** <term>rod</term> <note>original: "κανόνος" (kanonos); referring to the lever arm</note> to the lower part, the **box** <term>box</term> <note>original: "πυξίς" (pyxis); a small container or cylinder used here as a plunger or cap</note> **n x** will be raised. But if we release the end **l**, the box will be lowered and will surround the pipe **h e z**. And the air within [the box], having no way out because of the liquid surrounding the pipe **h e z**, no longer allows [the water] to be carried through the mouth **h**. 

<note>This describes a "water seal" or "air lock." When the box is lowered into the water, it traps a pocket of air over the top of the pipe. Because the air cannot escape through the water, it creates pressure that prevents water from entering the pipe, effectively turning off the faucet.</note>

When we again press the end **l** toward the lower part, the **spout** <term>spout</term> <note>original: "κρουνός" (krounos)</note> will flow. +

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for a pneumatic automaton. While the previous page introduced the concept of figures that appear to drink or blow using hidden pipes and vessels, this page describes the specific internal plumbing and valve mechanism (the smērismation and platismation) required to compress air and forcibly eject liquid, completing the "blowing" or "drinking" illusion.</meta>

Another **pipe** <term>pipe</term> <note>original: "σωλήν" (sōlēn); a tube for conveying fluid or air</note>, labeled **k l**, should be passed through the base. Let the **k** end of it be attached to the point through which we wish the water to be **discharged** <note>original: "ἀποτίζεσθαι" (apotizesthai); literally 'to be drunk up,' referring here to the liquid being drawn out of the reservoir</note>. The **l** end should be kept away from the bottom of the vessel just enough to allow for a plentiful flow. This **k** end must be fitted with a **small valve** <term>small valve</term> <note>original: "σμηρισμάτιον" (smērismation); a precision-ground airtight stopper or plug</note>, by which its opening—being quite narrow—will be closed off. 

Therefore, after first pouring **liquid** <note>original: "ὑγρόν" (hygron)</note> into the base through a certain hole (which we must seal tight after filling), if we close the **k** opening and blow **air** <note>original: "ἀέρα" (aera)</note> through the **e z** **pipe**, the air blown in will push aside the **small plate** <term>small plate</term> <note>original: "πλατισμάτιον" (platismation); a small disk or flap acting as a one-way check valve</note>. As the air inside the base is forced downward, it will seal the small plate shut again. 

Then, when the **small valve** is opened after a short time, the **compressed** <note>original: "πιληθεὶς" (pilētheis); referring to air that has been packed or condensed under pressure</note> air will squeeze out the liquid in the base through the **k** opening with great force, until all the liquid is discharged and the air returns to its <unclear>natural</unclear> state. For it works <unclear>whenever</unclear> they compress [the air] within it so as not to have an empty void.

<meta>This page continues the technical description of pneumatic apparatuses from Hero of Alexandria's "Pneumatica."
