If the liquid that has been poured out is removed, it flows. However, if a **pause** <note>original: "λείμματος" (leimmatos); an interval, gap, or cessation in the flow</note> occurs, the poured liquid no longer flows. Let it always be divided into halves and the [amount] will be poured out. But once a pause occurs, it no longer flows until it is filled again. For let there be a certain **vessel** <note>original: "ἀγγεῖόν" (angeion)</note> **A B**, containing within it **curved** <term>curved</term> <note>original: "καμπυλοειδεῖς" (kampyloeideis); bent or arched in shape like a bow</note> **siphons** <term>siphons</term> <note>original: "σίφωνας" (siphōnas); tubes used to convey liquid through atmospheric pressure</note> labeled **C D E**, which are hidden within the **body** <note>original: "γαστέρι" (gasteri); literally 'belly' or internal cavity</note> of the vessel. Let one leg of each siphon be positioned at the **bottom** <note>original: "πυθμένι" (pythmeni)</note> of the vessel, while the other legs lead outside, arranged into various **spouts**. To the outer ends of these siphons, let **receiving cups** <term>receiving cups</term> <note>original: "ἄντλα" (antla); small vessels or buckets used to collect or draw liquid</note> labeled **F G H** be attached, the bottoms of which should be separated from the openings by <unclear>just enough</unclear> for a **clearance for flow** <term>clearance for flow</term> <note>original: "διάρρυσιν" (diarrysin); the space required for liquid to exit the pipe and enter the cup without being blocked</note>. Again, let these be enclosed by another vessel, serving as a **base** labeled **K L M N**, which has its own spout **X**.

And siphon **C**, because of its smaller **curvature** <note>original: "κυρτότητα" (kyrtotēta); the arch or bend of the pipe</note>, should have its bend right at the bottom. Siphon **D** should have its bend at half the height of the **A B** vessel, and siphon **E** should be slightly above that. If we then pour water into vessel **A B**, it will, as expected, flow through siphon **C**, since its curvature is right at the bottom. But if, after an interval, the vessel is filled, the liquid no longer flows through **C** until...

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of Proposition 14 from Hero of Alexandria's Pneumatica. It details the physics of why the flow of a siphon stops when air enters the system, a phenomenon known today as an airlock.</meta>

...the <unclear>remaining part</unclear> <note>original: "λειφθὲν" (leiphthen); that which is left over or remains</note> is full of water. But the rest of the **siphon** <term>siphon</term> <note>original: "σίφωνος" (siphōnos); a tube for moving liquid by atmospheric pressure</note> will be full of **air** <note>original: "ἀέρος" (aeros)</note>. Therefore, when we pour in the liquid again, it will not pass through **siphon C**, because air exists in **siphon C**, between both the liquid that is about to be poured out and the **water** <note>original: "ὕδατος" (hydatos)</note> already within **vessel C** <note>original: "ἀγγείῳ" (angeiō); here referring to a specific internal container or pipe section marked 'C'</note>,

->[Technical Diagram]<-

<note>The diagram illustrates a complex hydraulic system. A large circular vessel (A, G) sits atop a base (K, L, M, N) containing internal outlets (P, X, R). Inside the main vessel, three distinct siphons are shown at varying heights, with the central siphon (D) being the highest. This setup allows for the "magic" effect where water only flows from certain spouts depending on the fill level and the presence of air or liquid in the tubes.</note>

### The construction of a **cupping glass** <term>cupping glass</term> <note>original: "Σικύας" (Sikyas); a medical instrument used to create suction on the skin</note> which operates **without fire** <note>original: "ἄνευ πυρός" (aneu pyros); Hero is describing a mechanical vacuum rather than the traditional method of heating air to create suction</note>.

Let the cupping glass be **A B G D**, which is **nipple-shaped** <note>original: "θῆλαι" (thēlai); a common ancient description for the rounded, conical shape of medical suction vessels</note>, having a **diaphragm** <term>diaphragm</term> <note>original: "διάφραγμα" (diaphragma); an internal partition or dividing wall</note> **H E** in the middle. Through the bottom of this, let it be fitted with a **ground-joint** <term>ground-joint</term> <note>original: "σμηρίσματι" (smērismati); a precision-fitted, airtight mechanical joint</note>. Let there be a small pipe **Z H** attached to it, and another pipe **Th K** on the inside. Let this have certain well-made **openings** <note>original: "τρυπήματα" (trypēmata); bored holes</note> **L M** which are on the outside of the cupping glass. Let the mouths of these same tubes be open. Let the outer part of **Th K** be blocked off and reach down to the bottom. Let there also be a ground-joint **N X** beneath the diaphragm **H E**, similar to the one at the bottom, which already has the same well-made holes leading into the interior part of the cupping glass, and continues along with **H E**.

<vocab>
**Cupping glass** (Σικύα): A medical device for suction.
**Without fire** (ἄνευ πυρός): Using mechanical pneumatics instead of heat.
**Diaphragm** (διάφραγμα): A partition dividing the device's internal volume.
**Ground-joint** (σμήρισμα): An airtight fitting made by grinding two surfaces to fit perfectly.
**Small pipe** (αὐλίσκος): A narrow tube for air or liquid flow.
</vocab>

### Construction of the Partitioned Vessel

For let there be a **vessel** <note>original: "ἀγγεῖον" (angeion)</note> **α β**, having a **tap** <note>original: "κρουνὸν" (krounon)</note> **γ** near the bottom. Let it be divided at the neck by the **partition** <note>original: "διαφράγματι" (diaphragmati)</note> **δ ε**. Underneath the partition, let a **tube** <term>tube</term> <note>original: "σωλήνα" (sōlēna); here referring to a conduit for air or liquid</note> **ζ η** be <unclear>suspended</unclear>, around which another tube is placed, separated from the partition by a distance sufficient to allow for the passage of water, just as in the case of **air-tight siphons** <note>original: "πνικτῶν διαβητῶν" (pniktōn diabētōn); these are 'choked' siphons which stop flowing when an air hole is blocked</note>. 

And again, let there be another **tube** <term>tube</term> <note>original: "σωλήν" (sōlēn)</note> **θ κ** passing through the partition, which extends above the partition by a very small amount at point **π ρ**, while the other end is adjusted to the taps at **λ μ**. And in this same manner, let another **tube** be placed around it, standing a short distance away from the partition. 

Let the **vessel** also have a **small pipe** <note>original: "αὐλίου" (auliou); a small vent or air-tube</note> **ν** located before the partition. If, therefore, while the taps are closed, we pour in **wine** <note>original: "οἶνον" (oinon)</note>, it will pass into the **vessel body** <note>original: "κύτος" (kytos)</note> through the **tube** **ζ η**. The air within will escape through the **small pipe** **ν**. 

If, however, we block the **small pipe** and open the taps, the liquid that was left behind in the **tube** **θ κ** will flow out from **λ μ**, while the liquid in the **vessel body** will flow out from the tap at **γ**. If, then, while the tap **γ** is flowing, we pour in a **cup** <note>original: "κύαθον" (kyathon); a standard Greek measure for wine, approximately 45ml</note> of water...

### The Mechanism of the Altar

**W**hen an **altar** is lit, the fire, acting through **compressed air** <note>original: "pneuma"; literally 'breath' or 'spirit,' but here referring to the expansion of heated air</note>, causes the **dragons** <note>original: "drakontas"; likely ornamental serpent figurines acting as whistles</note> to hiss. This pipe branches into the **ζ η** hearth <term>tubes</term> <note>original: "sōlēnas"</note>. One branch, **ε ζ**, leads to the mouth of the dragon, while the other branch, **ε η**, leads into a **wine-receiving vessel** **κ λ**. 

The bottom of this vessel is not the full base of a **small figure** <note>original: "zōdiou"; a common term in Hero's work for an automaton or decorative figurine</note>, but is attached to the partition of the **κ λ** vessel in a **cup-shaped manner** <note>original: "charkēeidōs", likely a corruption of "karchēsieidōs," referring to a karchēsion or specialized Greek drinking cup</note>. There is another pipe, **ε ν ξ**, which likewise reaches into another wine-receiving vessel **ο**, also cup-shaped. Both are connected to the bottoms of the vessels. Let there be in each of the wine-receptacles **curved siphons** <note>original: "siphōnes"</note>, labeled **π**, **ρ**, **σ**...

<page-num>52</page-num>

And the **wine** <term>vessel</term> <note>original: "angós"; a container or jar</note> has its outlets within the wine itself. This was already described in the previous diagram regarding the **wine-receptacles** <note>original: "oinodocheíōn"; specifically vessels designed to hold wine in a pneumatic system</note>. It is through these components that the movement of the **figurines** <note>original: "zōdíōn"; small, animated mechanical figures, often shaped like people or animals</note> is achieved by means of **compressed air** <note>original: "pneumátōn"; referring here to the pressure generated by heated air</note>. 

When one intends to light the fire, they should first introduce a small amount of water into the **tubes** <note>original: "sōlēsin"; internal pipes or conduits</note>, so that the **tubes** do not crack from the dry heat of the fire; the fire should be kindled with wood in such a way that the air does not leak out. Then the **heated air** <note>original: "pneûma"</note> from the fire, having been mixed with the water <note>likely creating a rudimentary steam or expansion effect</note>, will flow through the **tubes** toward the hands [of the figures]. And through these hands, the wine is poured out.

# How to make a tap flow according to a specific measure from a vessel's base.

On a certain **base** <note>original: "basei"</note>, given a **vessel** <note>original: "angeiou"</note> containing water or wine, when the **tap** <note>original: "krounou"</note> is opened and the hand is removed, the device will make the tap flow according to a specific measure. For example, whatever volume we desire, whether a half-**kotyle** <note>original: "hēmikotylion"; an ancient Greek unit of volume roughly equal to 0.15 liters or a small cup</note>, or sixty-four [units], or a **kotyle**. In general, the device regulates itself according to this intended measure. 

Let *alpha-beta* be the **vessel** from which the wine will be poured out. And let there be a **tap** *eta* in it, positioned near the **bottom** <note>original: "pythmena"</note>. For ventilation <note>original: "anapnoēn"; literally "breathing," referring to the air intake needed to allow liquid to flow</note>, divide the **neck** with the **partition** *epsilon-zeta*. Through the *epsilon-zeta* **partition**, let there be a **tube** *eta-theta*, which is at a distance from the **bottom** of the vessel, <unclear>the woman ... to dry out</unclear> <note>original: "ho gynē ... diaphrysein"; this phrase is likely a corruption in the manuscript, possibly referring to a specific sealant or a missing mechanical component</note>. 

And let the **base** situated beneath the vessel be *kappa-lambda-mu-nu*. Let there be another **tube** *xi-omicron*, placed a short distance from the **partition**, but passing through the **base**. Let there also be water in the **base**, so that it covers the **mouth** <note>original: "stomion"</note> of the **tube** *xi-omicron*. Let there also be a **rod** <note>original: "kanōn"; a straight-edge or control bar</note> *pi-rho*, having one half-part inside the **base** and the other half outside, circular—

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of a hydraulic regulator. The previous page established the physical setup of the vessel, the beam (kanōn), and the internal pressure system. ...that the wine will not flow, because air cannot enter from anywhere. However, as we lower the *rho* end of the **beam** <term>kanōn; the horizontal balance lever</term>, a part of the **clepsydra** <term>klepsydra; in this context, a small floating regulator vessel</term> will be raised out of the water.

If, once the clepsydra is filled again, we lower the *rho* end further than before, the liquid held up in the clepsydra will flow out for a longer time. Consequently, more liquid will flow from the tap. If the entire clepsydra is raised up, much more will flow. 

To ensure we do not lower the *rho* end of the beam at random, there shall be a smooth **pointer** <note>original: "cheir"; literally "hand"</note> at *phi-pi*, which moves along the outer part of the beam at *rho-chi*. When the pointer is near *rho*, it will raise the clepsydra only a little; when it is further away, it will raise it less <note>This likely refers to the mechanical advantage or the travel distance of the lever arm</note>. 

By **experiment** <term>peira; practical trial</term>, having found the specific **quantities** <term>metra</term> we wish to flow from the *eta* tap, let us make **notches** <term>entomas</term> on the *rho-chi* beam and **labels** <term>epigraphas</term> for those quantities. Thus, whenever we wish a certain amount to flow out, by setting the pointer to that mark and releasing the mechanism, the liquid will flow.

## Construction of a vessel designed so that, at first, mixed wine flows. When we wish to pour in water, both the water and the pure wine flow out, and then mixed wine again.

Let the **drinking horn** <term>rhyton; a conical or horn-shaped vessel</term> be the area marked *alpha-beta*. Let the neck be divided by a partition, and let *delta* be this **partition** <term>diaphragma</term>.

<margin>Let the neck be partitioned</margin>

Let a certain **double-channel** <term>diaulos; a two-way pipe or duct</term> be led from the point of flow, with the **nozzle** <term>rhymation; a small spout</term> having the point *eta* at the **constrictions** <note>original: "brachesi"; Hero's text usually refers to the narrowest part of a vessel's neck, though the manuscript reading is slightly unclear</note>. Let there be another channel in the rhyton under the partition, marked *theta*. 

If—while blocking the outlet of *theta*—we pour in the **mixed wine** <term>krama; wine diluted with water, the standard drink of antiquity</term>, it will pass into the body of the rhyton through the nozzle at *eta*. 

However, when we release the outlet, the mixed wine will flow out, because **displaced air** <note>original: "aeros antipiptontos"; literally air "falling against" or rushing in to replace the escaping liquid</note> enters through the small channel at *theta*. 

But when we block the channel *theta* and pour in pure water, the mixed wine will not flow, because it has no **air supply** <note>original: "paradosin tou aeros"; literally a "delivery" or "handing over" of air to relieve the vacuum</note>, but the pure water [will flow instead]. Whenever we release...

## How to again produce mixed wine from both.

Suppose there is a vessel standing on a certain base, having a **spout** <term>krounon; a tap or faucet</term> located above the **bottom** <term>pythmenos</term>. When water is poured into it, at one time pure water flows out, at another time mixed wine, and at another time only **pure wine** <term>akraton; undiluted wine</term>.

Let the vessel be marked **alpha-beta**, standing on a base and having a spout at **gamma**. Let there be an opening, **epsilon**, situated above the bottom of the vessel. Let the **neck** <term>trachēlos; the narrow upper part of the vessel</term> be divided by a **partition** <term>diaphragma</term> marked **epsilon-zeta**. 

<margin>Let the neck be partitioned</margin>

Through this partition, let a **small pipe** <term>sōlēniskos</term>, marked **eta-delta**, be let down so that it projects slightly above the partition into the upper part, but remains a distance away from the bottom of the vessel so that water does not flow through it <note>The pipe acts as an air-trap or a conduit depending on the water level</note>. 

Let there also be another pipe for the **air-vent** <term>anapnoē; literally "breathing hole"</term> of the vessel, marked **eta-kappa**, on the outside. Beneath this, let a reservoir for pure wine be placed, marked **mu**. Furthermore, let there be a tiny **hole** <note>original: "trypēma"</note> in the **epsilon-zeta** partition, marked **nu**.

Now, these things being so, if we pour water into the vessel through the neck, the portion of water that remains around the projecting part of the small pipe will stay in the neck. However, the water that overflows the pipe will be carried down into the **body** <term>kytos; the main hollow chamber of the vessel</term>. This continues until the water reaches the **sigma** opening of the spout, at which point pure water will flow out.

But when we wish the spout to flow just as in the... <note>The text breaks off here; Hero likely intended to reference a previous mechanism or start the explanation of how the air-pressure from the water forces the wine from the hidden reservoir to create the mixture.</note>

...through the **siphon** <term>diabētos; a bent tube used to move liquids over an edge using gravity and pressure</term>, so that the **pure wine** <term>akraton; undiluted wine</term> will again be held back in the same vessel, and a mixture will flow out. When the water has been exhausted, then the pure wine alone will flow. Now, the first water [poured] near the **epsilon-zeta** partition will not be shut off. But when all the water around the partition has flowed out through the **nu** **hole** <note>original: "trypēma"</note>, then the air will be blocked, it will pass into the **air-vent** <term>anapnoē; literally "breathing hole"</term>, and nothing further will flow.

## Concerning a vessel full of wine, and having a spout,
beneath which is placed a **drinking-cup** <term>potērion; a standard ancient Greek vessel for liquids</term> measured for exactly as much wine as must flow into it. Let the **wine-reservoir** <term>oinodocheion; a large storage container</term> be the vessel **alpha-beta** <note>original: "α β"</note>, having a spout **gamma-theta** <note>original: "γ θ"</note>. The mouth of the spout near **eta** <note>original: "η"</note> has its upper surface **channeled** <term>ochetōton; formed like a conduit or grooved pipe</term>, so that when a **small drum** <term>tympaniou; a circular disk or float</term> **epsilon-zeta** <note>original: "ε ζ"</note> is placed upon it, it contains the water.

On the left, a tall framing rod is labeled "η", "θ", "κ", "λ". A base mechanism below features labels "ξ", "σ", "μ".</image-desc>

Let there be a vertical **rod** <term>kanonion; a straight rule or small bar used in mechanical alignments</term> **eta-theta** <note>original: "η θ"</note> fixed upon the water, from which another **bent rod** **kappa-lambda** <note>original: "κ λ"</note> is attached.

Let there also be another **rod** <term>kanōn; a straight mechanical bar or lever</term> beneath the base of the vessel, labeled **mu-nu** <note>original: "μ ν"</note>, which is pivoted around the point **xi** <note>original: "ξ"</note>. Let two other rods, **pi-rho** <note>original: "π ρ"</note>, be attached to this, moving upon **pins** <term>peronais; small pivot bolts or axles</term>. The arrangement is such that when the end **mu** <note>original: "μ"</note> of the rod is **depressed** <term>katabibasthentos; pushed down by the weight of the cup</term>, and the **small drum** <term>tympaniou; the float/valve disk</term> **epsilon-zeta** <note>original: "ε ζ"</note> is **raised** <term>epairomenou; lifted upward</term>, the spout is opened and the liquid flows out; but when the pressure is released, the spout is closed again. 

Therefore, let a **drinking-cup** <term>potērion</term> be placed upon the rod **mu-nu**, into which we wish to pour a **pre-measured amount** <term>prosmetrēmenon; an exactly apportioned quantity</term>, and no less. And let the liquid be ready at the spout.

There should also be a certain **sliding rod** <term>leiantēs; a smooth, polished rod used as a guide or counterweight</term> **eta-theta** <note>original: "η θ"</note>, which is able, by its position, to be received into the protruding part of the rod **mu-omicron** <note>original: "μ ο"</note>. Whenever the water <note>Hero uses "water" and "wine" somewhat interchangeably in these pneumatic proofs</note> reaches the upper part **eta** <note>original: "η"</note>, the spout will be opened and the liquid will flow into the cup. 

As the cup becomes heavy with the liquid, the **smooth rod** <note>original: "λεία" (leia), a shorthand for the leiantēs</note> will move upward again and close the spout. In order for the pre-measured amounts to flow out correctly, let a volume equal to a **kotyle** <term>kotylē; a standard Greek measure of volume, roughly 0.24 liters</term> be put into the cup. Let the liquid flowing from the spout be received in another vessel, and let the smooth rod be moved until the spout no longer flows. 

At this point, let a mark be made upon the rod, and let the word **kotyle** <note>original: "κοτύλη"</note> be inscribed there. Likewise, marks should be made for a **half-kotyle** <term>hēmitylion; half of a kotyle measure</term> and for two kotylai. We shall continue making these measurements as far as we wish. Through this similar [method]...

# Construction of a Purification Wheel

**The** construction of a chamber having a revolving **bronze wheel** <term>chalkeion; literally "bronze thing," here referring to the ritual wheel</term>, which is called a **purification vessel** <term>hagnistērion; a ritual device for cleansing</term>. For it is the custom of those entering into **temples** <note>original: "hiera" – sacred precincts</note> to turn this wheel. Let it be constructed in a distinct manner, so that no harsh sound is produced. And from the **water vessel** <note>original: "udatiou"</note> itself...

<note>Hero of Alexandria is describing a mechanical "holy water" dispenser. In ancient Greek temples, these wheels were often placed at the entrance; when a worshipper turned the wheel, a internal mechanism (likely a valve or a series of buckets) would dispense a small amount of water for ritual washing of the hands.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the technical description of a constant-level wine mixer and introduces the "Purification Wheel," a common mechanical fixture at the entrance of ancient Greek temples.</summary>

Let these components possess a **spool** <term>exeliktran; a drum or windlass for winding cord</term>. The opposite part of the mechanism should remain stationary. Now, let a **cord** <term>spartos; a rope made of twisted fibers, often esparto grass</term> be wound around the **spool**.

...being easily able to turn. Situated next to the points **S E KH** <note>original: "ϛ ε χ"; labels corresponding to a technical diagram</note> of the **cover** <note>original: "mēta keryphou"; likely a variant spelling referring to the lid or top casing mentioned previously</note>, positioned near the spokes of the **drum** <term>tumpanon; a gear-wheel</term> and engaged with the drum **P**.

It will then follow that when the **wheel** **TH** is turned, the **cord** <term>spartē</term> is wound around the **spool** <term>exeliktran</term>, lifting the **plunger** <term>pnigeus</term> away from the **water wheel**. When the heavy plunger then descends back into the water, it produces a **sound** <term>ēchos</term> as the air is squeezed out <note>Hero often used this displacement of air to blow through flutes or whistles, creating "miraculous" sounds</note>. At the same time, the **cover** is rotated back to its original position through the rotation of the drums.

Regarding the **siphons** <term>siphōnes</term> that are submerged in vessels: they flow until their liquid reaches a certain level, specifically until the surface is level with the outer **mouth** <term>stomion</term> of the siphon. 

Suppose it is required that we make the siphon stop flowing whenever we wish, even while it is in the middle of discharging. Let the vessel be labeled **A B**, in which there is a siphon **G D E**. This siphon has an internal length that is bent back, labeled **E Z H**. 

Let there also be a straight **rod** <term>kanonion</term> positioned around **H Z K**. To this rod, on the outer side, let a piece **L M** be attached. From this, another rod **M N** is suspended and moves along with it; at point **N**, it has a curved vessel capable of fitting over the **bend** <term>anakamptē</term> **Z H** of the siphon. 

Let the rod **L M** have a **weight** hanging from point **L**. When the vessel **TH** <note>referring to the cup-like attachment</note> fits over the siphon's bend, it stays above the **mouth**, and the siphon flows. But whenever we wish for it to no longer flow, we remove the weight at **L**...

# Animals that can be cut through and drink.

Let there be a tube, <term>a-b</term> <note>original: αβ</note>, located in its mouth, and another tube, <term>g-e</term> <note>original: γη</note>, in the neck; this latter tube extends down into the interior of the legs, which are arranged in a circle. In the middle of both of these, let there be a "male" <term>cylinder</term> <note>A solid rod or shaft that fits into a corresponding hollow part</note>, <term>e-z</term>. To this cylinder, let <term>toothed bars</term> <note>original: κανόνες ὀδοντωτοί (kanones odontōtoi); these function like modern gear racks</note> be attached, marked <term>h</term>. Above one of these bars, let there be a portion of a <term>toothed drum</term> <note>original: τυμπάνου ὀδοντωτοῦ (tympanou odontōtou); a gear or cogwheel</note>, marked <term>k</term>; and below the bar <term>h</term>, let there be a similar portion of a toothed drum, <term>l</term>.

Above all the parts directed toward the neck, let the internal <term>cut</term> <note>The slit through which the theatrical knife passes</note> be thicker than the external one, featuring a larger spiral with three circles, <term>m, n, x</term>. As in the previous setup, there is a gap before the cut, which, together with the exterior part, allows for the regular movement of the drum. Let the circles be cut together at the point <term>x</term>, so that the second circumference is no longer a complete circle. 

Through the cut in the upper part of the second vessel, where the points <term>p, r, m, h</term> are located, and within the cut of the separated head, let a hollow curved part be fitted. This part should be wider at its base than at its outer edges, so that the <term>cone-shaped tube</term> <term>p-r</term> acts as a "female" socket, containing two sides of an inscribed hexagon in the same pattern.

<note>This complex arrangement of gears and sliding tubes allows a knife to pass through the animal's neck. As the knife enters, it pushes a rack that rotates the internal tube out of the way, while a secondary seal maintains the vacuum or water flow, allowing the animal to continue "drinking" without interruption.</note>

<summary>This page describes the intricate rack-and-pinion mechanism used to create the illusion of an animal drinking while its neck is being cut. It details the alignment of toothed gears and sliding cylinders that maintain a continuous seal for the liquid.</summary>

<keywords>automaton, hydraulics, gears, toothed drum, rack and pinion, pneumatics, Hero of Alexandria, illusion</keywords>

# $\dagger$ The Poimandres of Hermes Trismegistus $\dagger$

Once, when I began to meditate on the <term>beings</term> <note>original: ὄντων (ontōn) — things that truly exist or the nature of reality</note>, and my thought was greatly uplifted, while my bodily senses were held in check—much like those weighed down by sleep from a surfeit of food or from fatigue of the body—I thought I saw a being of immense size, beyond all limited measure, calling my name and saying to me: "What do you wish to hear and see, and having understood in your mind, to learn and know?" I said, "And who are you?" He said, "I am the <term>Poimandres</term> <note>original: ποιμάνδρης (poimandres) — typically translated as 'Shepherd of Men,' though it may also derive from an Egyptian phrase meaning 'Knowledge of Ra'</note>, the <term>Mind</term> <note>original: νοῦς (nous) — the divine intellect or higher consciousness</note> of absolute sovereignty. I know what you desire, and I am with you everywhere." I replied, "I wish to learn about the things that are, to understand their nature, and to know <term>God</term> <note>original: θεόν (theon)</note>. How I wish to hear!" He spoke to me again: "Keep in your mind all that you wish to learn, and I will teach you." Having said this, his form changed, and immediately everything was opened to me in an instant. I saw a boundless vision: everything had become <term>Light</term> <note>original: φῶς (phōs)</note>, mild and joyous, and I fell in love with the sight. But after a little while, a <term>Darkness</term> <note>original: σκότος (skotos)</note> began to descend...

->**Vocabulary List**<-
**Hermes Trismegistus:** Hermes the Thrice-Greatest.
**Poimandres:** The Shepherd of Men / Divine Mind.
**Mind (Nous):** Divine or supreme intellect.
**God:** The divine source.
**Light:** The symbol of divine revelation and life.
**Darkness:** The symbol of the material or chaotic world.

<summary>This page contains the famous opening of the Poimandres, where Hermes Trismegistus enters a meditative trance and encounters a divine being who promises to reveal the secrets of existence.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Poimandres, Divine Mind, vision of light, spiritual awakening, Greek philosophy, Hermeticism</keywords>

...[after a little while, there] came to be a certain part, both fearful and gloomy, coiling in sinuous folds. As <gloss>I</gloss> <gloss>myself</gloss> <gloss>watched</gloss>, I saw this darkness changing into a certain watery nature, unspeakably troubled, giving off smoke <gloss>to me</gloss> as if from a fire. It emitted a certain unspeakable, mournful sound; then a discordant cry was sent forth from it, which I likened to a voice of light.

Out of the light, <gloss>a certain</gloss> <term>Holy Word</term> <note>original: λόγος ἅγιος (logos hagios) — the divine reason or creative speech of God</note> descended upon that nature. Pure <term>Fire</term> <note>original: πῦρ (pyr)</note> leaped forth from the watery nature up into the heights; it was light, piercing, and active all at once. The <term>Air</term> <note>original: ἀήρ (aēr)</note>, being light, followed the <term>Spirit</term> <note>original: πνεύματι (pneumati) — meaning 'breath' or 'spirit,' here acting as a moving force</note>, ascending <gloss>both</gloss> <gloss>it</gloss> from the <term>Earth</term> <note>original: γῆ (gē)</note> and <term>Water</term> <note>original: ὕδατος (hydatos)</note> as far as the Fire, so that the Air seemed to hang from the Fire. 

But Earth and Water remained mixed together by themselves, so that the Earth was not visible separately from the Water. Yet they were kept in motion because of the spiritual Word that was heard moving over them. 

Then Poimandres said to me, "Do you understand what this vision means?" 

I said, "I shall come to know." 

He said, "That Light is I, your God, who existed before the watery nature that appeared out of the darkness. The luminous Word coming from the Mind is the <term>Son of God</term> <note>original: υἱὸς θεοῦ (huios theou)</note>." 

"What do you mean?" I asked. 

"Know it in this way," he said, "that which sees and hears within you..."

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>This page continues the speech of Poimandres (the Divine Mind) to the narrator. He describes how he protects the virtuous while leaving the wicked to an avenging spirit. The dialogue then shifts to the "ascent"—the mystical process of what happens to the human soul and body after death as it returns to the divine source.</meta>

After <gloss>these things</gloss>, the <term>Mind</term> <note>original: Nous — the divine intelligence personified as Poimandres</note> will stand aloof from the sensations of the body that assault it. Acting as a gatekeeper, it will shut the entrances against evil and shameful activities, cutting off those thoughts. But from the foolish, the wicked, the evil, the envious, the greedy, the murderers, and the ungodly, I remain far away. I yield their place to the <term>avenging spirit</term> <note>original: timōrō daimoni — a divine agent of retribution or punishment</note>, who, applying the sharpness of fire, pierces <unclear>him</unclear> through his senses. 

Furthermore, this spirit arms the man with even more lawless deeds so that he may face a greater punishment. Such a person never ceases <gloss>having</gloss> an insatiable desire for boundless appetites, struggling in the darkness; the spirit tortures him and increases the fire upon him more and more.

"You have taught me all things well, just as I wished, O Mind. But tell me further of the <term>ascent</term> <note>original: anodou — the journey of the soul rising back to the divine realm after death</note> that takes place."

To these things <term>Poimandres</term> <note>the "Shepherd of Men," the name of the divine Mind speaking here</note> said: "First, in the dissolution of the material body, you give up the body itself to change, and the form you once had becomes invisible. Your <term>character</term> <note>original: ēthos — your personal disposition or way of being</note> is surrendered to the spirit, becoming inactive. The bodily senses return to their own sources, becoming parts once again, and are reabsorbed into their original functions. And <term>anger</term> <note>original: thymos — high spirit, passion, or wrath</note> and <term>desire</term> <note>original: epithymia — physical craving</note> depart into the irrational nature."

<summary>Poimandres explains how the Divine Mind protects the holy while the wicked are punished by their own uncurbed desires; he then describes the post-death dissolution of the body and the return of human passions to the irrational world.</summary>
<keywords>Poimandres, Mind (Nous), ascent of the soul, divine punishment, dissolution of the body, senses, anger (thymos), desire (epithymia), avenging spirit</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the mystical instruction of Poimandres to the narrator. Following the description of the body's dissolution, it details the soul's ascent through the seven planetary spheres (the "harmony"), where it sheds various earthly vices before finally reuniting with the Divine. This passage is the climax of the Poimandres, establishing the core Hermetic goal: the "deification" of the human soul through knowledge.</meta>

And thus, the human <gloss>soul</gloss> rushes thereafter upward through the <term>harmony</term> <note>original: harmonias — the celestial system of the seven planetary spheres that governs fate and material life</note>. And to the first <term>zone</term> <note>original: zōnē — a layer or level of the heavens, traditionally associated with the Moon</note>, he gives up the energy of growth and waning. And to the second, the device of evils: craftiness, now rendered inactive. And to the third, the deception of desire, rendered inactive. And to the fourth, the arrogance of command, now stripped of greed. And to the fifth, unholy daring and the recklessness of audacity. And to the sixth, the evil strivings for wealth, rendered inactive. And to the seventh <term>zone</term>, the lie that lies in wait.

And then, stripped of the effects of the <term>harmony</term>, he enters the "eighth nature," possessing his own power. And with those who already exist there, he sings hymns to the <term>Father</term> <note>original: patera — the ultimate source of divinity</note>. Those present rejoice at his arrival, and having become <term>Powers</term> <note>original: dynameis — divine emanations or spiritual beings</note> themselves, they come to be in God. This is the good end for those who have attained <term>knowledge</term> <note>original: gnōsin — the deep, experiential insight into divine truth</note>: <term>to become divine</term> <note>original: theōthēnai — the process of deification or returning to a state of godhood</note>.

So now, why do you delay? Since you have received all these things, will you not become a guide to those who are worthy, so that through you the race of humanity might be saved by God?

After saying these things, Poimandres mingled with the <term>Powers</term>. And I, having given thanks and blessed the <term>Father</term> of all things, was sent forth by Him—empowered and taught the nature of the universe and the supreme vision.

<summary>This passage describes the soul's ascent through seven heavenly spheres to shed worldly vices, its entry into the eighth sphere of the fixed stars, and its final deification. The text concludes with the narrator being commissioned as a spiritual guide after his vision from Poimandres ends.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the private revelation of Poimandres to the narrator's public mission. Having been "deified" by the vision on the previous page, the narrator (identified traditionally as Hermes Trismegistus) now acts as a missionary, calling humanity away from material "sleep" and "drunkenness" toward spiritual "sobriety" and "knowledge."</meta>

...delay? Having received all things, should you not become a guide to those who are worthy, so that through you, the human race might be saved by God?

The <term>knowledge</term> <note>original: gnōsis — spiritual insight into the divine</note> of <term>piety</term> <note>original: eusebeias — right devotion or godliness</note> was proclaimed to those who lacked understanding.

O world! O peoples, <term>earth-born men</term> <note>original: gēgeneis — literally "born of the earth," referring to those who identify only with their physical, mortal nature</note>! You who have given yourselves over to drunkenness and sleep and to the ignorance of God: Be sober! Cease your drunken revelry, for you are spellbound by an irrational sleep.

And when they heard this, they gathered around me with one accord.

But I said: Why, O <term>earth-born men</term>, have you given yourselves over to death, when you have the power to partake in <term>immortality</term> <note>original: athanasias</note>? <term>Repent</term> <note>original: metanoēsate — a total change of mind or shift in consciousness</note>, you who have journeyed with error and shared in ignorance! Release yourselves from the dark light; partake of immortality, leaving corruption behind.

Then some of them, having mocked me, went their way, giving themselves over to the path of death. But others threw themselves at my feet and entreated to be taught. And I, having raised them up, became a guide to the human race, teaching them the words—how and in what way they shall be saved. 

And I sowed in them the words of <term>wisdom</term> <note>original: sophias</note>, and they were <gloss>baptized</gloss> <note>original: ebaphēsan — literally "dipped" or "dyed," suggesting a spiritual soaking or initiation</note> in the <gloss>water of life</gloss> <note>original: ambrosiou hydatos — literally "ambrosial water," the divine sustenance that confers immortality</note>.

And when evening came and the sun’s radiance began to set entirely, I commanded them to give <term>thanks</term> <note>original: eucharistein — to offer a prayer of gratitude</note> to God. And having completed the thanksgiving, each man turned to his own bed.

But I [recorded] the benefit...

<summary>Following his mystical vision, the narrator becomes a prophet, urging humanity to wake from the spiritual "sleep" of the material world and accept the path to immortality. <meta>This page marks the conclusion of the first tractate (Poimandres) and the beginning of the second tractate of the Corpus Hermeticum. The transition shifts from a visionary hymn of praise to a philosophical dialogue concerning the nature of motion, space, and the divine.</meta>

And I believe and I bear witness that I am entering into life and light. Blessed art thou, Father. Your human being wishes to join you in holiness, just as you have handed over all authority to him. +

<image-desc>A flourished decorative red initial Theta 'Θ' within a circle, positioned in the left margin next to the heading of the second book.</image-desc>

### Hermes to Tat: A General Discourse +

Is it not true, Tat, that everything that is moved is moved within something and by something? But let us speak of the divine—not merely what has been pointed out by others, but the divine itself. It is essential in its nature. But if it is so, how <gloss>in essence</gloss> <unclear>will it be</unclear>... 

Nothing is beneath it. For that which is situated there is a primary <term>intelligible</term> <note>original: noēton — refers to things that can only be understood by the mind or intellect, rather than perceived by the physical senses</note> not intelligible to itself. For the thing perceived by the mind falls under the awareness of the one who perceives it. 

But God is not an object of mind to himself. <unclear>For the same thing</unclear> as that which is thought is understood by himself. What does this show us, except that what is situated there exists through him? In this way, "place" is an object of the mind. Not God, but place. And if he is also understood as God, it is not as a place, but as a <term>receptive energy</term> <note>original: energeia chōrētikē — the active, divine power that "contains" or provides the space for all existence to occur</note>. 

Everything that is moved is moved not within something that is moving, but within something that stands still. And that which moves the object also stands still, for it is impossible for the mover to be moved along with the object. 

"How then, O Thrice-Greatest, are the things here moved along with the things being moved? For you said that the <term>wandering spheres</term> <note>original: sphairas planōmenas — the planets, which appear to "wander" across the sky compared to the fixed stars</note> are moved by the <term>unwandering sphere</term> <note>original: aplanous sphairas — the outermost heaven or the firmament of fixed stars</note>." 

Are these not motions, O Asclepius? No, they are stationary motions. For they do not move in the same way, but in opposition to one another. And this opposition keeps the resistance of the movement stationary.

--- Page 166 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the beginning of Tractate II. Having established that everything that moves must move within something that is stationary (space/place), Hermes now explains the paradox of how motion is generated through resistance and stability.</meta>

And resistance <note>original: antitypia — the physical "push-back" or solidity that provides the leverage necessary for movement to occur</note> is a manifestation <gloss>a state of rest</gloss> from which motion arises. For example, as with the wandering <unclear>spheres</unclear> <note>original: planōmenōn — the planets, which were thought to move in spheres</note> moving in a uniform way, they encounter the fixed sphere <note>original: aplanē — the outermost sphere of the stars which does not "wander"</note> with an opposing force; this is the counter-resistance. This moves around the very opposition of the superiority <unclear>moving</unclear>; it is impossible for it to be otherwise.

For take these "Bears" <note>The constellations Ursa Major and Ursa Minor. In the northern hemisphere, these are circumpolar, meaning they circle the celestial pole and never "set" below the horizon.</note> that you see, which neither set nor rise. They turn around the same point—do you think they move, or do they stand still?

"They move, O Trismegistus."

"And what kind of motion is it, O Asclepius?"

"The kind that <unclear>turns</unclear> around the same point."

But this revolution—this movement around itself—is the same as a motion that is accomplished by a state of rest. For the movement "around it" prevents any movement "beyond it." And because the movement "beyond it" is prevented, the movement "around it" stands firm. In this way, the opposing movement is stable, being supported by that very opposition. 

I will provide an example for your eyes to see. I will describe it using earthly creatures: take the swimmer, for instance. For while the water flows away, the resistance of the feet and the hands becomes a state of rest for the human, preventing them from being swept away with the water.

"You have given a very clear example, O Trismegistus."

Therefore, all motion is moved within a state of rest, and is moved by rest. Thus, the motion of the cosmos and of every material living being is not accomplished by things outside the cosmos...

<note>original Greek vocabulary provided in OCR: resistance (antitypia), rest/stability (stasis), motion/carrying (phora), Thrice-Greatest (Trismegistos), Asclepius, movement (kinēsis)</note>

<summary>Hermes explains to Asclepius that all movement requires a stationary point of resistance to exist, using the constellations of the Bears and the act of swimming as primary examples. He concludes that the motion of the universe is ultimately supported by stability.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Asclepius, celestial mechanics, Ursa Major, physics of motion, resistance, stability, planetary spheres</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue of Corpus Hermeticum II. Following the discussion on resistance and the motion of the heavens, Hermes begins to explain the interior source of motion and the impossibility of a true "void."</meta>

...happens to occur. But it is moved by the things within, pushing toward the outside, either through the **soul** or **spirit** <term>pneuma: a Greek term for "breath" or "spirit," often viewed in this period as a subtle substance that transmits life and motion</term>, or some other **incorporeal** thing. For the body of a living thing does not move itself, nor does body move as a whole, even if it is inanimate <note>original: apsychon — literally "without soul" or "lifeless"</note>.

"How do you say this, O Trismegistus? Are things like wood and stones and all other things—the living <note>The OCR reads "empsycha" (living/ensouled), though the context of wood and stones suggests "apsycha" (inanimate) was likely intended in the original manuscript.</note> things—not bodies that move, O Asclepius?"

"Not at all. That which moves the inanimate thing is not a body, but rather that which moves both the carrier and the carried. Therefore <unclear>in sleep</unclear> <note>The text here is compressed; it refers to how the soul remains active even when the body is at rest.</note> that which moves. You see, then, that the soul is burdened because it alone bears the body. And while it is clear that things being moved are moved within something and by something, it is in that <insert>incorporeal</insert> space that they must move, O Trismegistus."

"It is <unclear>upward-tending</unclear> <note>original: anōpheres. This may be a scribal variant for 'asōmatos' (incorporeal), which is found in standard versions of this text.</note>, O Asclepius. For nothing among the things that exist is **empty**. Only non-being is empty, for it is a stranger to existence. That which exists could never be empty."

"Are there then no empty things, O Trismegistus? Such as a chest, or jars, or a box? An empty vessel, or a hollow <unclear>hand</unclear>, and all other such things?"

"Alas for your great error, Asclepius! You are ignorant that those things which you think are empty are actually the most full and greatest.

"How do you mean, O Trismegistus?"

"**Air** is a body, and this..."

<vocab>soul, spirit/breath, incorporeal, empty/void, existence, air</vocab>

<summary>Hermes explains that motion originates from an incorporeal source (soul or spirit) rather than the physical body itself. He further argues against the existence of a "void," claiming that what we perceive as empty vessels are actually filled with the "body" of air.</summary>
<keywords>incorporeal, motion, soul, pneuma, void, air, Asclepius, Hermes Trismegistus</keywords>

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page concludes the dialogue from Corpus Hermeticum II. It continues the discussion on the nature of space, the elements, and the divine Mind (Nous) as the container of all things.</meta>

...the body, nor from those things within it <insert>it extends and fills every place</insert> body, not mixed from other bodies. Therefore, the body is full, from which comes the motion of the air. 

"The things, then, that everyone says are empty—you call them 'hollow' rather than empty. For they are actually full of air and **spirit** <term>pneuma: the vital breath or subtle substance that the ancients believed permeated the universe and gave life</term>." 

"An irrefutable argument, O Trismegistus. Air is a body. And this body extends through all things that exist and fills every place. What then shall we call the place in which the whole universe is moved?"

"Incorporeal, O Asclepius."

"What then is the Incorporeal?"

"It is **Mind** <term>nous: the divine intellect or higher consciousness</term> and **Reason** <term>logos: the rational principle or 'word' that orders the cosmos</term>. It already contains itself within itself, free from every body, unerring, unaffected by the physical and intangible. It stands stable within itself, capable of containing all things and the savior of all that exists. Its rays are the **Good**, the **Truth**, the **Archetypal Light** <note>original: archetypon phōs — the original 'blueprint' or source of light from which all physical light is derived</note>, and the **Archetypal Soul**."

"God, therefore, is not like any of these things <unclear>existing</unclear>; He is the cause of their very existence, both for the whole and for every individual part of all things that are. For no other essence exists that is more permanent; but all <unclear>these things</unclear> come into being from things that exist, and not from things that do not exist. For things that do not exist have no nature that allows them to be able to become anything, but rather they are simply the inability to be. Conversely, things that exist do not have a nature that allows them to never be. What then do you say? That they are never to be?"

<summary>In this concluding segment of the dialogue, Hermes explains to Asclepius that 'void' does not exist, as all space is filled with air and spirit, and that the ultimate container of the universe is the incorporeal Mind of God.</summary>
<keywords>Mind (Nous), Reason (Logos), Pneuma, Incorporeal, Archetypal Light, Non-being, Continuity of Existence</keywords>

<page-num>80</page-num>
<meta>This text follows the discussion from the previous page regarding God as the source of existence. It corresponds to the beginning of Book VI of the Corpus Hermeticum, which focuses on why "The Good" exists in God alone and nowhere else.</meta>

God, therefore, is likewise the cause of being. He is not **spirit** <term>pneuma: the vital breath or subtle substance</term>, but the cause that spirit exists. He is not light, but the cause of illumination. Hence, one must worship God by these titles <term>original: prosegoriai — names or formal addresses</term>, for they belong to Him alone and to no other—not to any of the other so-called gods, nor to men, nor to **demons** <term>daimons: intermediary spirits, not necessarily evil in the Greek worldview</term>. 

For He cannot even be named <unclear>according to</unclear> [human names], as He is the only God. If this word "only" is used, it is <unclear>because</unclear> <unclear>under</unclear> His name He is the container of all things. Of His glory—for He is both body and soul—no place exists capable of containing Him. 

**The Good**: for the Good is as great in magnitude as is the existence of all things that are, consisting of bodies and incorporeal things, and sensible things <term>sensible: things perceivable by the physical senses</term> and **intelligible** things <term>intelligible: things known only by the mind or intellect</term>. This is the Good. This is God. 

Do not, therefore, call anything else "good," for you would be impious; nor call God anything else ever except "the Good," for that would be impious. Or again, [do not call] God anything else ever but only the Good, for that is once more impious <note>The manuscript here repeats the warning against misusing the title "Good," likely a scribal emphasis on the exclusivity of the term</note>. 

By word, then, all things are called "the good" by everyone; yet not all things are [good] in the eyes of everyone. For this reason, God is not understood by all. Rather, through ignorance, people give the name "good" to certain gods and even to certain men, though these can never be good, nor could they ever become so. For the Divine is the most true and inseparable, just as God Himself is. All the other gods, therefore, are immortals honored with the title of "God."

<vocab>αἴτιος: cause, προσηγορία: title, ἀγαθόν: the Good, οὐσία: essence/being, ἀσέβεια: impiety</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that "The Good" is a title exclusive to God, who is the cause of all existence, light, and spirit. It warns that applying the name "good" to humans or lesser deities is a form of spiritual ignorance and impiety.</summary>
<keywords>The Good, nature of God, impiety, intelligible vs sensible, divine names, monotheism, Hermeticism</keywords>

--- Page 170 ---
<meta>This text concludes the discussion from Libellus VI regarding God as "the Good" and transitions into Libellus III, known as the "Sacred Discourse of Hermes." It maintains the dialogue between Hermes and his disciple Asclepius.</meta>

God is the Good, not by status <note>original: "kata timēn" — referring to an assigned honor or rank</note> but by nature. For the nature of God is one: the Good. There is one genus for both from which all things come; for the Good is the one who gives all that is and receives nothing. God, therefore, gives all things and receives nothing. Thus, God is the Good, and the Good is God.

The other title <term>original: prosegoria — a formal name or address</term> is that of "the Father," again because He is the maker of all things; for it is the part of a father to make. Therefore, the most earnest and pious concern in life for those who are wise <term>original: euphronousin — those of sound mind or right thinking</term> is the begetting of children <term>paidopoia: the act of producing offspring</term>. It is considered the greatest misfortune and impiety for someone to depart from the human race childless. Such a person pays a penalty after death to the **demons** <term>daimons: here, intermediary spirits who act as executors of divine justice</term>.

The punishment is this: the soul of the childless person is condemned to a body that possesses the nature of neither man nor woman. This state is something cursed by the Sun. Therefore, O Asclepius, do not pray to have sympathy for any childless person; on the contrary, laugh at his misfortune, knowing what kind of punishment awaits him.

Let these things, and such as these, be said to you, O Asclepius, as a foreknowledge of the nature of all things.

<meta>Recto of folio 81. This page continues the "Sacred Discourse" (Libellus III) of the Corpus Hermeticum, picking up mid-sentence from the previous page's introduction. The text describes a Hermetic cosmogony—an account of how the universe and life were created through divine light, the separation of elements, and the birth of the seven planetary governors.</meta>

...of the cosmic course, Nature is the active force, and Necessity is its fulfillment <note>original: "telos" — the end goal or completion</note>. 

And there was a renewal. There was an infinite darkness in the abyss, and water, and a subtle, intelligent spirit <term>pneuma: a "breath" or "spirit" that was thought to permeate the universe</term> existing within chaos by divine power. Then, a holy light was sent forth, and the elements were solidified out of the moist substance upon the dry ground <note>original: "hyp' ammō" — literally "under sand," referring here to the solid earth or the "dry" principle separating from the "moist"</note>. 

And all the gods were given form by nature, as if within a seed. While all things were yet undefined and unformed, the light elements were separated toward the heights, and the heavy things were founded upon the moist sand, having been consumed by fire <unclear>from there</unclear> and suspended, so as to be carried by the spirit. 

And the heavens appeared in seven circles. The gods were seen in their active states, appearing along with all their celestial signs. And the cosmos was articulated together with the gods within it. The outer circumference was enveloped by air, moving in a circular course, carried by the divine breath.

Each god, through his own power, brought forth what was commanded of him. And there came to be four-footed beasts, and creeping things, and creatures of the water, and winged birds, and every seed-bearing seed, and grass, and the greenery of every flower—all containing within themselves the seeds of rebirth <term>paliggenesia: a natural or spiritual regeneration; the ability for life to renew itself</term>. 

These were created for the life and birth of humanity, to the end that they might attain the true knowledge <term>epignōsis: a deep, experiential or "higher" knowledge</term> of divine things and bear witness to the works of nature. And there was a multitude of men, created to have dominion over all things under heaven, and to have knowledge of good things, so that they might "increase in increasing" <note>This phrase "increase in increasing" (en auxēsei) echoes the biblical "increase and multiply," but here it refers to the expansion of both the human race and human understanding</note>.

<summary>This page describes the Hermetic creation myth, detailing how light emerged from chaos to separate the elements and form the seven planetary spheres and all biological life.</summary>
<keywords>Cosmogony, Chaos, Seven Circles, Elements, Paliggenesia, Epignosis, Creation of Man</keywords>

--- Page 172 ---
<meta>Verso of folio 81. This page concludes the third book of the Corpus Hermeticum (The Sacred Discourse) and begins the fourth book, known as "The Mixing Bowl" or "The Monad." It transitions from a description of the cosmic cycles to a philosophical dialogue between the sage Hermes Trismegistus and his disciple, Tat.</meta>

And they were to multiply in multitude. And every soul in the flesh [multiplied] through the course of the gods, through the circular orbits <term>periporeia: the revolving paths of the stars and planets that dictate the timing of life</term>. This was for the contemplation of the heavenly gods, and the course of the gods, and the eternal gods, and the works of the gods.

And the energy of nature provides signs of good things, bearing divine powers. As fate is carried along, it allows for the discerning <term>gnōmonein: to act as a pointer or a judge, like the gnomon of a sundial</term> of good and evil things.

And to discover every craftsmanship of good things. The beginning of their life and wisdom was found in those things measured out by the course of the divine circles.

And it shall be dissolved into that which shall be: great memorials of artistic works upon the earth, leaving behind a fading <note>original: "amaurosis" — a darkening, dimming, or obscuring through the passage of time</note> of years for every generation of unmindful flesh and of the fruit of the seed. And every work of art that has been diminished shall be renewed by necessity and by the renewal of the gods, in the numerical course of nature's cycle. For the divine is the entire cosmic mixture <term>synkrasis: a blending or tempering together of different elements</term> renewed by nature; for in the divine, nature also is established. +

### + Hermes to Tat: The Mixing Bowl, or the Monad. +

**E**ver since the Creator <note>original: "demiourgos" — the craftsman-god who fashions the physical world</note> made the whole cosmos, not with his hands but by the Word <note>original: "logos" — meaning the divine reason, speech, or plan that orders the universe</note>, you should understand Him as being present, and always existing, and having made all things, and being only One. Having created by his own will; for indeed, He is God.

<summary>This page concludes a discourse on how human life and craftsmanship are bound to the celestial cycles of the gods, before transitioning into a new teaching where Hermes explains that the world was created through the divine 'Logos' rather than physical labor.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, cosmic cycles, rebirth, Hermes Trismegistus, Tat, Monad, Logos, Creator, craftsmanship, fate</keywords>

<meta>Verso of folio 82. This page continues the "Mixing Bowl" discourse from the Corpus Hermeticum, where Hermes explains the difference between those who possess the higher Mind (Nous) and those who possess only human Reason (Logos).</meta>

These shared in the **knowledge** <term>gnosis: spiritual or intuitive knowledge of the divine</term> and became perfected humans by receiving the **Mind**. But as many as missed the message of the herald, these are the ones who possess only **reason** <note>original: "logikos" — meaning they have the faculty of speech and logic, but lack the higher spiritual intuition</note>, not having received the **Mind** because they are ignorant of why they were born and by whom they were made. 

Their **senses** <term>aistheseis: physical perceptions and feelings</term> are very much like those of irrational animals. Having their temperament mixed in **passion** <term>thumos: the spirited or emotional part of the soul, often associated with fire</term> and in **anger**, they do not marvel at anything worthy of vision, but give their attention only to the **pleasures** and desires of the body. Because of these things, they believe that man was born for this purpose alone.

But as many as shared in the gift from God, these, O Tat, in comparison to the others, are immortal instead of mortal. They embrace all things in their own **Mind**—the things on earth, the things in heaven, and if there is anything above heaven. Having raised themselves so high, they saw the **Good**. And having seen it, they considered their **stay** <term>diatribe: a passing of time, a delay, or a way of life</term> here on earth to be a misfortune. Having looked down upon all things, both bodily and incorporeal, they hasten toward the One and Only.

This, O Tat, is the **knowledge** <term>episteme: systematic or certain divine knowledge</term> of the **Mind**: an abundance of divine things. And to understand God is itself a divine thing—this is the mystery of the mixing bowl.

"I too wish to be **baptized** <note>original: "baptisthenai" — here meaning to be immersed or dipped into the spiritual vessel of Mind described on the previous page</note>, O Father."

"Unless you first hate your body, my child, you cannot love yourself. But having loved yourself, you will have **Mind**. And having the **Mind**, you will also partake in the **knowledge**. How..."

<vocab>gnosis, aistheseis, thumos, orge, hedonai, agathon, diatribe, episteme</vocab>

<summary>Hermes distinguishes between 'rational' humans who live by their senses like animals and 'perfected' humans who receive the gift of the Mind, allowing them to perceive the Divine. The passage concludes with Tat's desire to be 'baptized' in the spiritual mixing bowl, though Hermes warns this requires a detachment from the physical body.</summary>

<meta>Recto of folio 83. This continues the dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus and his son Tat from the "Mixing Bowl" discourse (Corpus Hermeticum IV). The text transitions from describing the vision of the Good to the necessity of choosing between the material and spiritual worlds.</meta>

"Are you saying this, O father?"

"For it is impossible, O child, to be concerned with **both** at once—that is, with things mortal and things divine. For since there are two kinds of things, the bodily and the incorporeal, in which the **mortal** and the **divine** consist, the **choice** <term>hairesis: a choice or a taking for oneself; the root of the word 'heresy'</term> of one or the other is left to the person who wishes to choose. It is not possible to choose both; where a choice must be made, the one that is neglected makes manifest the **energy** <term>energeia: active power or operation</term> of the other.

The choice of the better, then, is not only the most praised from the beginning—having as its success the **deification of man** <note>original: "apotheōsai" — the process of a human becoming divine or god-like</note>—but it also demonstrates **piety** <term>eusebeia: right conduct or devotion toward the divine</term> toward God. But the choice of the lesser destroys the man. Even so, it does nothing to offend the divine, except for this one thing: just as **processions** <note>The author compares people living only for the body to a public parade (pompa) that fills the street but has no real business there</note> passing through our midst are unable to accomplish anything themselves, yet they hinder the path of others, in the same way these people merely **parade** through the world, led along by the pleasures of the body.

Since these things are so, O Tat, the things that come from God have been and will be available to us. But let the things that depend on us follow after, and let them not fall behind. For God is **blameless** <note>original: "anaitios" — a famous Platonic concept that God is not the cause of evil; humans choose it</note>; it is we who are the causes of evils, preferring these things instead of the good.

Do you see, O child, through how many **bodies** we must pass, and through how many **choirs of spirits** <term>daimones: intermediate spiritual beings, not necessarily evil</term>, and through what a succession and course of **stars**, so that we may hasten toward the One and Only—"

<sig>84</sig>
**Vocabulary:** **Immortal**, **mortal**, **choice**, **deify**, **piety**, **blameless**, **spirits**, **stars**.

<summary>Hermes explains that one cannot pursue both physical pleasure and divine wisdom, asserting that while God provides the Good, humans are responsible for the evil they choose. He concludes by describing the soul's long journey through the celestial spheres to return to the One.</summary>

--- Page 176 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Mixing Bowl" or "Monad" discourse (Libellus IV) from the Corpus Hermeticum. In the previous section, Hermes explained that God is not the cause of evil; rather, evil arises from the choice of the material over the divine. He compared those living only for the body to a parade that blocks the street but accomplishes nothing. This page concludes the fourth book and transitions into the nature of the "Beginning" and the "Monad."</meta>

...we speak because of that which is **Good** <term>agathon: the ultimate moral and spiritual reality, often equated with God in Hermetic thought</term> and **Infinite**. And you said that these things are also **without beginning** <note>original: "anarchon"</note>. To us, however, the **Beginning** <term>archē: the first principle, source, or origin of all things</term> seems to extend only as far as what can be known.

In this Beginning, therefore, **knowledge** <term>gnōsis: experiential or spiritual insight, rather than intellectual data</term> comes to be. It provides us with the starting point of what is to be known. Let us, then, seize this Beginning.

And let us travel quickly through all things. For it is entirely **crooked** <note>original: "skolion" — meaning winding, difficult, or indirect</note> to leave behind our present habits and turn back toward the ancient and original ways. For the things we can see bring us delight, but the **invisible** <note>original: "aphanē" — things that do not appear to the physical eyes</note> things create disbelief. Evils are most visible to us.

But the **Good** is invisible to visible things. There is no form or shape belonging to it. For this reason, it is like itself, but unlike all other things. For it is impossible for the **incorporeal** <term>asōmaton: that which lacks a physical body or material substance</term> to be made manifest to the body. This is the difference between the like and the unlike.

And the unlike suffers a deficiency compared to the like. But the **Monad** <term>monas: the "One" or the indivisible source; in Pythagorean and Hermetic thought, the number from which all other numbers emerge</term>, being the beginning and root of all, is indeed the Beginning. It possesses nothing of a beginning prior to itself. A beginning must come from nothing, or from itself, since it is the beginning of all others. This is so because it does not even have a beginning by chance.

The Monad, therefore, is the beginning and contains every contained thing, while being contained by nothing. Since it becomes the container of all things, it is not created by anything, but all other things come from it. For all things that are **generated** <term>gennēta: things brought into existence or "born" into the material world</term> are incomplete, and **indivisible** <note>The scribe here wrote "adiaireta" (indivisible), though other manuscripts of this text read "diaireta" (divisible), describing the fragmented nature of material things</note>, and capable of growth and decrease.

<summary>Hermes explains that while the Good is invisible and lacks physical form, it is the "Monad" or source from which all material things originate. He warns that the path to spiritual knowledge is difficult because humans are naturally drawn to the visible world rather than the invisible reality.</summary>
<keywords>The Good, Monad, Beginning (Arche), Knowledge (Gnosis), Incorporeal, Generated things, Hermeticism</keywords>

If you gaze upon it accurately and understand it with the eyes of the heart, believe me, child, you will find the way to the things above. Or rather, the image itself will guide you. <margin>K</margin> **A**nd consider this discourse, O Tat, through this explanation, so that you are not uninitiated in the name of the One who is greater than any name. But you, understand how that which seems **invisible** <note>original: "aphanēs" — that which does not appear to the physical eyes</note> to the many will become most manifest to you. For it would not exist if it were invisible. For everything that is **manifest** <note>original: "phainomenon" — that which appears or is visible</note> is **created** <note>original: "gennēton" — born, generated, or having a beginning</note>; for it has appeared. But the invisible always exists. For that which is manifest does not need [to appear], for it always is. But the **Good** makes all things manifest, being Himself invisible, just as He is always existing. Making things manifest, He is not Himself made manifest. He is not Himself created. But He is in the **imagination** <term>phantasia: the power of making things appear to the mind or senses</term> of all imaginations. For imagination belongs only to created things. For imagination is nothing else but **becoming** <note>original: "genesis" — the process of being born or coming into existence</note>. But the One is clearly **unbegotten** <term>agennētos: uncreated, having no source or beginning</term>, and thus beyond imagination and invisible. But all things...

<meta>This page continues the dialogue of Libellus V. Hermes Trismegistus is explaining to his son, Tat, that although God is invisible to the physical eye, His presence is clearly visible through the order and beauty of the created world.</meta>

...of appearances, He is manifest through all things and in all, and especially to those whom He Himself wishes to appear. You then, O child Tat, pray first to the Lord and Father, the Only, and the One, from whom the One comes. May you find favor, so that you may be able to **perceive** <term>noēsai: to understand or grasp with the spiritual mind rather than the physical senses</term> such a great God, and that He might shine even a single **ray** <term>aktis: a beam of divine light or influence</term> of Himself into your intellect. For understanding alone sees the **invisible** <note>original: "to aphanes" — that which is hidden from sight</note>, since understanding is itself invisible. 

If you are able, He will appear to the eyes of your mind, O Tat. For the Lord is without envy and appears through the whole world. You can see understanding and grasp it with your very hands, and behold the image of God. But if that which is within you is invisible to you, how shall He manifest Himself to you through your own eyes? 

If you wish to see Him, consider the **sun** <note>original: "hēlios"</note>; consider the course of its race; consider the **stars** and their **order** <term>taxis: the systematic and divine arrangement of the cosmos</term>, and how they maintain that which is according to their arrangement. Who is the one who defined this order, its **number** <note>original: "arithmos"</note>, and its place? 

The sun is the greatest of the **heavenly gods** <note>original: "ouranioi theoi"</note>, to whom all the other heavenly gods yield as if to a king and a ruler. And this one, so great—greater than the earth and the sea—holds above himself and has stars smaller than himself revolving around him. Out of respect for whom, or fearing whom, O child, does he do this? Each of the stars in heaven does not follow a course that is identical or equal to another. Who is it that assigned to each its order and the magnitude of its course?

<summary>Hermes instructs Tat on how to perceive the invisible God through the intellect, using the orderly movement of the sun and stars as evidence of a divine Architect.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue of Libellus V. Hermes Trismegistus has been describing the visible order of the heavens as proof of the invisible God. He moves from the sun and stars to the constellation of the Bear and the fundamental elements of the physical world.</meta>

...having defined it. This **Bear** <note>original: "arktos" — referring to the constellation Ursa Major</note> is she who turns around herself and carries the whole **world** <term>kosmos: the universe conceived as an ordered and beautiful system</term> along with her. Who is the one who possesses this **instrument** <note>original: "organon" — the cosmic mechanism</note>? Who is the one who has cast the boundaries around the sea? Who is the one who fixed the earth upon its foundation?

For there is someone, O Tat, who is the **Maker** <note>original: "poiētēs"</note> and **Master** <note>original: "despotēs"</note> of all these things. For it is not possible for place, or number, or measure to be maintained without the one who made them. For all **order** <term>taxis: the divine arrangement of things</term> is [impossible without a creator]; even that which is out of place or without measure is not apart from a master, my child. For if the **disordered** <note>original: "ataktou" — the transcription likely meant "disordered" rather than "allatou"</note> is in need of one who provides, this one must be the master of order. Even that which is inferior has not yet received its own order.

But if it were possible, who could have raised up the god of the air? For there is a God of the earth and the heaven, so that the **infinite** might be thus. Consider the pouring out of the sea, the flow of the rivers, the looseness of the air, the sharpness of fire, the course of the stars, and the order of the heaven and its revolution around itself.

O most fortunate vision, my child! To behold all these things in a single **moment** <note>original: "rhopēn" — literally a turn of the scale or a quick movement</note>: to see the **Unmoved** through the things that move, and the **Invisible** becoming visible through the very order of the world which He has made.

And this is the world of order. Or do you wish to behold Him through mortal things, those on the earth and those in the deep? Consider, my child, the **man being fashioned** <note>original: "dēmiourgoumenon"</note> within the womb, and the fashioning...

<summary>Hermes explains to Tat that the mathematical precision of the stars and the elements proves the existence of a Master Creator. He emphasizes that the invisible God becomes visible through the "fortunate vision" of the world's perfect order.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discourse of Libellus V, moving from the macrocosm of the stars and elements to the microcosm of the human body. Hermes is using the biological complexity of man as further evidence for a divine Creator.</meta>

...of the structure, you have precisely beheld the reason. If you wish to see the **Craftsman** <term>dēmiourgos: a divine artisan or creator who fashions the physical world</term> of this beautiful and divine **image** <note>original: "eikona" — an image or likeness</note> of man: who is the one who outlined the eyes? Who is the one who hollowed out the nostrils and the ears? Who is the one who opened wide the mouth? Who is the one who stretched out and bound the nerves? Who is the one who channeled the veins? Who is the one who made the bones firm? Who is the one who wrapped the flesh in skin? Who is the one who separated the fingers? Who is the one who widened the base for the feet? Who is the one who brought the most precious parts out into the open and hid the <unclear>shameful</unclear> <note>original: "aischra" — referring to the private or reproductive organs</note> parts?

Look how many **crafts** <note>original: "technai" — skills or arts</note> come from a single **matter** <note>original: "hylē" — the raw material of the universe</note>. And how many works come from a single outline. And all are most beautiful, and all are measured, yet all are in variety. Who made all these things? What mother? What father? If not the invisible God, who by His own will fashioned all things.

And while no one says that a statue or an image comes to be without a **sculptor** <note>original: "andriantopoios" — literally a maker of statues of men</note> or a painter, can it be said that this **workmanship** <note>original: "dēmiourgēma"</note> came to be without a Craftsman? Oh, what great blindness! Oh, what great...

<summary>In this passage, Hermes Trismegistus uses the anatomical complexity and purposeful design of the human body to argue for the existence of an invisible Creator. He compares the divine creation of man to the work of a sculptor or painter, suggesting it is irrational to believe such a complex "image" could exist without an artisan.</summary>
