--- Page 181 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse of Libellus V from the Corpus Hermeticum. Hermes is concluding his argument that the invisibility of God is a result of His being too manifest to be seen by physical eyes alone, much like the sun is too bright to look at directly. He moves into a profound hymn regarding the nature of the Creator as the "All-in-All."</meta>

...acts impiously. Let it be considered a great ingratitude. Never, O child, [think to separate] the **Creator** <term>dēmiourgos: a divine artisan or creator who fashions the physical world</term> from His creations. Rather, He is even greater than a name <note>The transcription here reads 'the darkness of the name,' which is a variation from standard texts that simply state He is 'greater than a name.'</note>. So great is the **Father** of all things. He is the leader, and it is impossible for His work to be absent from Him. 

And if you compel me to say something even bolder: His **essence** <term>ousia: the fundamental nature or substance of a thing</term> is to conceive all things and to make them. And just as it is impossible for anything to come into being without one who makes it, so it is [impossible for Him not to exist] <unclear>not to be</unclear>, unless He is always making all things. 

He is in heaven, in the air, on the earth, and in the deep. He is in every part of the **world** <term>kosmos: the universe conceived as an ordered and beautiful system</term>. He is in every part of the whole. He is in that which is and in that which is not. For there is nothing in all of these that He is not. He is both the things that are and the things that are not. For the things that are, He has made manifest; but the things that are not, He keeps within Himself. 

This is the One who is greater than any name; this is the invisible One; this is <image-desc>Small decorative floral motif in right margin</image-desc> the most manifest One. He is the One contemplated by the **Mind** <term>nous: divine intellect or the "eye of the soul"</term>; He is the One visible to the eyes. He is the **bodiless** <term>asōmatos: without a physical body; purely spiritual or conceptual</term> and yet the many-bodied. Or rather, He is every essence of body. For there is nothing that He is not. For everything that exists—He is that as well. 

And for this reason, He has all names, because they belong to the one **Father**; and for this reason, He has no name at all, because He is the **Father** of all things. Who then is able to bless You, concerning You or toward You? And where should I look to bless You? Upwards? Downwards? Inwards? Outwards?

<summary>Hermes explains that the Creator's essence is the perpetual act of creation, making Him both invisible (unmanifest) and the most visible (the physical world). He concludes that because God is everything, He possesses every name and yet no name can truly encompass Him.</summary>
<keywords>Creator, Essence, Manifestation, Father of All, Panentheism, Divine Names, Mind (Nous)</keywords>

--- Page 182 ---
<meta>This page continues the concluding hymn of Libellus V and transitions into Libellus VI of the Corpus Hermeticum. The previous page established that God is both hidden and most manifest, existing in all things that are and are not. This page completes that thought by identifying God as the totality of existence before beginning a new discourse on the nature of "The Good."</meta>

For there is no manner of being, nor any place around Him, nor any other thing among all that exists. All things are in Him; all things are from Him. You give all things and receive nothing; for you possess all things and there is nothing that you do not hold. 

When shall I sing your praise? <note>original: "ὑμνήσω" (hymnēsō) — to sing a hymn or song of praise</note> For it is impossible to grasp your season or your time. And for what shall I sing? For the things you have created, or for the things you have not? For the things you have revealed, or for the things you have hidden? And why shall I sing to you? As if I belonged to myself? As if I had something of my own? As if I were another apart from you? 

For you are whatever I may be. You are whatever I may do. You are whatever I may say. For you are all things, and there is nothing else that is not you. You are everything that comes into being, and you are that which does not. You are the **Mind** <term>nous: the faculty of divine intellect or spiritual perception</term> that is perceived. You are the **Father** who fashions the world. You are **God** in action. You are the **Good** <term>agathos: the ultimate moral and ontological perfection</term> who performs all things. For of **matter** <term>hylē: the physical substance of the universe</term>, the most subtle part is air; of air, the **soul** <term>psychē: the life-force or animating principle</term>; of the soul, the mind; and of the mind, God.

<image-desc>A red ink decorative initial or stylized floret mark in the left margin next to the heading.</image-desc>
->† That the Good is in God alone, and nowhere else.<-

The **Good**, O **Asclepius** <note>In the Hermetic tradition, Asclepius is a primary disciple of Hermes Trismegistus, representing a seeker of wisdom. He is named after the Greek hero/god of medicine.</note>, exists in nothing else except in God alone. Or rather, God Himself is the Good eternally. And if it must be an **essence** <term>ousia: the fundamental nature or "beingness" of a thing</term> in this way, it is the essence of all motion, yet it is void of nothing <note>The text here is somewhat fragmentary in the original transcription, suggesting the Good is self-sufficient and does not need external movement.</note>. It is self-contained, possessing an **activity** <term>energeia: the active power, operation, or "at-work-ness" of a thing</term> that is neither in want nor in excess—a most bountiful provider.

...at the beginning of all things. For he who provides everything is **Good** <term>agathos: the ultimate moral and ontological perfection</term>.

### That the Good is in God Alone, and Nowhere Else

Because I say "all things," it follows that the Good is always. This quality belongs to no one else, except to God alone. For he is not in need of anything, such that he might become evil by desiring to possess it. Nor does he lose anything that exists—the loss of which would cause him to grieve (for grief is a part of **evil** <term>kakia: vice, badness, or moral failing</term>).

Nor is there anything stronger than him, which he would have to approach in conflict. Nor is there any companion to wrong him, which would cause him to fall in love <note>original: "ἐρασθήσεται" (erasthēsetai) — here implying a possessive or reactive desire triggered by an external relationship</note> with such a thing. Nor is there anything disobedient that could make him angry, nor anyone wiser whom he might envy. Since none of these things exist in his **essence** <term>ousia: true being or substance</term>, what remains but the Good alone?

For just as no evil is found in such an essence, likewise the Good will be found in nothing else. In all other things, everything else exists—in the small and in the large, both in individuals and in the Great Living Being <note>The "Great Living Being" refers to the Cosmos itself, viewed as a single, animated organism.</note> itself, the greatest and most powerful of all.

For **passions** <term>pathos: literally "that which happens to one"; it refers to being acted upon by external forces, leading to suffering, emotion, or change</term> fill all created things, because the very nature of **becoming** <term>genesis: the process of birth, creation, and material existence</term> is itself passive and subject to change. Where there is passion, the Good is nowhere. Where there is the Good, there is not a single passion. For where there is day, there is no night; and where there is night, there is no day.

Hence it is impossible for the Good to be found within the world of becoming; it belongs only to the **unbegotten** <term>agennētos: that which is eternal and has no beginning</term>. Just as a participation in all things is granted to **matter** <term>hylē: the physical substance of the universe</term>, so it is with the Good. To that which truly exists, the Good...

<vocab>beginning, evil, passion/suffering, becoming/creation, unbegotten, matter</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that "The Good" is an exclusive attribute of God because God lacks the needs, fears, and conflicts that define material existence. It establishes a dualism where the "passions" of the physical world (becoming) are fundamentally incompatible with the stable perfection of the Good.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Libellus VI of the Corpus Hermeticum. The previous page established that the "Good" exists only in God’s essence. ...the cosmos. On its own, the cosmos is good only insofar as it acts as a creator <note>original: "ἐν τῷ μέρει τοῦ ποιοῦντος" — implies the world is "good" only in its functional role of generating life, not in its moral essence</note>. But in all other respects, it is not good. For it is subject to **passions** <term>pathos: the state of being acted upon by external forces, leading to change or suffering</term> and it is in constant motion, and it is the creator of things that are themselves subject to suffering.

In this world, all things are called "good" only by **comparison** <term>sygkrisis: a relative judgment where something is called good merely because it is less bad than something else</term>; the good is merely stored within it. For where the evil is greater, there the good is said to be. But the good in this place possesses only the smallest fraction. Therefore, it is impossible for the Good here to be pure of **evil** <term>kakia: vice or moral corruption</term>. For here, the good is tainted by evil. Once it is corrupted, it is no longer good; and when it ceases to be pure, it becomes evil.

The Good, therefore, exists only in God, or rather, God himself is the Good. So, Asclepius, do not look for the name of the "Good" among human beings, for its reality is nowhere to be found among them. It is impossible. For a material body—which is hemmed in on all sides by vice—cannot contain it. It is trapped in pains, in sufferings, in desires, in angers, in deceptions, and in foolish opinions. 

And the **worst of all** <term>kakiston: the superlative of bad; the most wretched thing</term>, Asclepius, is that in each of these things I have mentioned, people here believe the greatest good is to be found. The unsurpassable evil is **gluttony** <term>gastrimargia: literally "belly-madness"; the base drive for material consumption</term>, which is the provider of all other evils. It is the **error** <term>planē: a wandering away from the truth; spiritual delusion</term> and the absence of the Good.

But the Good is [not] here. Yet I give thanks to God, who put into my mind even this much **knowledge** <term>gnosis: direct, spiritual insight or revelation</term> regarding the Good: that it is impossible for it to exist within the world. For the world is the fullness of...

<summary>The text explains that the material world is inherently corrupted by "passions" and material needs, meaning that what humans call "good" is only a relative, diluted version of the true divine Good. Hermes teaches Asclepius that true Goodness exists only in God, and material desires like gluttony are the primary obstacles to recognizing this truth.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the sixth book (Libellus VI) of the Corpus Hermeticum, a collection of sacred texts attributed to Hermes Trismegistus. ...of vice. But God is the source of the Good, or rather, the Good is the very essence of God. For the heights of all things beautiful dwell around His very essence; they appear purer and most transparent. Perhaps these essences are even His own. For one must dare to say, O Asclepius, that the essence of God—if indeed He can be said to have an essence—is the **Beautiful**. 

But the Beautiful and the Good cannot be grasped among the things in this world. For all things that fall under the reach of the eye are **phantoms** <term>eidōla: images, reflections, or mere representations of a deeper reality</term>. They are like shadow-paintings. But the things that do not fall under the eye are the true realities, especially the essence of the Beautiful and the Good. Just as the eye cannot see God, so it cannot see the Beautiful or the Good. For these are complete and perfect parts of God—uniquely His own, intimate, inseparable, and most beloved. 

Either God Himself is the lover of these things, or all things are in love with God. If you are able to conceive of God, you will conceive of the Beautiful and the Good: that which is exceedingly joyful and exceedingly radiant, shining forth from God. For that **darkness** <note>original: "σκότος" (skotos). In this mystical context, "darkness" refers to the "Divine Dark"—a light so bright it appears as darkness to human eyes, or the incomprehensible mystery of the Divine.</note> is incomparable, and that Good is **inimitable** <term>amimēton: something so unique and perfect it cannot be copied or simulated</term>, just as He is Himself. 

Therefore, as you conceive of God, so also conceive of the Beautiful and the Good. For these are **unshared** <term>akoinōnēta: things that cannot be participated in or held in common with others</term> by any other living beings, because they are inseparable from God. If you seek after God, you are also seeking after the Beautiful. For there is only one road that leads to them: **piety joined with knowledge** <term>gnosis: a direct, experiential realization of divine truth</term>. 

Wherefore those who are ignorant—having <unclear>obstructed</unclear> the path of piety—have dared to call both evil and good...

### On the Soul and the Body

Now, my child, we must speak concerning the **soul** <term>psykhe: the animating life-force or spiritual essence</term> and the **body** <term>sōma: the physical form</term>. <gloss>12</gloss> For God, in His act of creation, made the **soul** <gloss>14</gloss> to be immortal, existing through manifold activity, <gloss>15</gloss> the composition of the body, and its duration. <gloss>17</gloss> For God is not subject to death; indeed, death does not even exist for Him. Rather, "death" is merely a thought and a name, <gloss>or</gloss> a title given to that which is "immortal." <gloss>19</gloss> It is either a hollow occurrence or, as is said, death <note>original: "θάνατος" (thanatos)</note> is named by the removal of the first letter of "immortal" <note>original: "ἀθάνατος" (athanatos). The author is making a linguistic argument: if you take the 'a' (the 'not' prefix) away from 'not-death' (immortal), you are left with the word 'death.' He argues this is a subtraction of language, not a reality of nature.</note>. 

For death is associated with <gloss>21</gloss> **destruction**, yet nothing within the **cosmos** <term>kosmos: the ordered universe</term> is ever truly destroyed. If the cosmos is a "second god" and an immortal living being, it is impossible for any part of an immortal living being to die. And all things within the cosmos are parts of the cosmos—most especially **man**, who is the rational living being. 

For before all things there is the **Creator** <term>demiurgos: the craftsman or maker of the world</term> of the whole, who is God: truly eternal, everlasting, and unbegotten. But the **second god** <note>In Hermetic philosophy, the physical Universe itself is considered a "Second God," created in the image of the First God to manifest His divinity in material form.</note> is the one made by Him in His own image; he is sustained, nourished, and made immortal by Him, living forever as if by his own father. 

For the "ever-living" differs from the "eternal." The eternal was not brought into being by another; or if it was, it was certainly not brought into being by itself. Rather, it is always becoming. For the **Eternal** <term>aidios: that which exists outside of time</term> is the "All" which exists alongside the eternal, but the Father Himself is eternal from Himself. The cosmos, however, has become eternal and immortal through the Father. For the "All" lay as the underlying substance of **matter** <term>hyle: the raw, unformed material of the universe</term>, for the Father of the body—

<margin>⸎</margin> ### On Understanding and Perception. That the Beautiful and the Good exist in God alone, and nowhere else. +

<margin>Χ ⸎</margin> **Y**esterday, O Asclepius, I delivered the "Perfect Discourse." <note>original: "τὸν τέλειον λόγον" (ton teleion logon). This refers to a specific Hermetic text known as the *Asclepius* or the *Perfect Sermon*, which discusses the nature of the divine and the role of humanity.</note> Now, I consider it necessary as a follow-up to that work to also discuss the discourse on perception. For perception <term>aisthēsis: physical sensation or sensory awareness</term> and intellect <term>noēsis: mental intuition or spiritual understanding</term> seem to have a difference, in that the former is material, while the latter is essential <note>In this context, "essential" refers to the "ousia" or the true substance/being of a thing beyond its physical form.</note>. But to me, they both seem to be united and not divided—at least <gloss>within human beings.</gloss> For in irrational animals, perception is united with nature, but in humans, it is united with intellect. Now, Mind <term>nous: the divine or higher intellect</term> and understanding differ as much as God differs from divinity. For divinity comes into being through God, and understanding through the Mind. They happen, then, to be siblings <gloss>being</gloss> of Speech <term>logos: can mean word, reason, or divine discourse</term>, or instruments of one another. For neither is speech uttered without understanding, nor does understanding manifest without speech. Thus, both perception and understanding flow together into the human being for one another's benefit.

<summary>This page explores the hierarchy of existence—God, Cosmos, and Humanity—while introducing a philosophical distinction between physical perception and intellectual understanding, which are uniquely unified in human beings.</summary>

Those who live with the good act according to their essence, keeping watch over themselves. 

And the Creator <term>demiourgos: the divine artisan or craftsman of the universe</term> of all things, while creating, made all things like himself. These things, having become good <gloss>those</gloss> in the use of their activity—yet cosmic decay or disorder <note>original: "φθορά" (phthora), meaning corruption or the natural process of wearing away</note>, intruding day by day, makes them grow old.

<summary>This page describes the isolation of the "pious" individual who, though persecuted by society, uses spiritual knowledge to transform all experiences into good. It also distinguishes between "material" humans and "essential" humans based on their relationship with divine understanding.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the two types of humans—those led by material vice and those led by essential goodness—and expands into a cosmology explaining how the physical world acts as a living instrument of the Divine will.</meta>

In some, vice stains; in others, goodness purifies. 
And the cosmos <term>kosmos: the world viewed as an ordered, harmonious system</term> is likewise; for goodness possesses a certain movement within it.

And the bodies which are appropriate to it are easily dissolved <note>That is, they are subject to decay and change.</note>. For some are made of earth; some of water; some of air; and some of fire. All of these are composite <note>original: "σύνθετα" (syntheta). These are things made of multiple elements, which explains why they can be broken down or "dissolved."</note>.

And some are greater, while others are simpler. The heavier things are greater, while the lighter things are more subtle. Such, then, is the nature of the world. And the world is as if within the world. And the world...

Still others give life through the spirit <term>pneuma: the breath of life or divine essence</term>. <gloss>And others</gloss> receive those that have grown weary <note>original: "κεκμηκότα" (kekmēkota). And fittingly so. For let me not say that He does not possess all things. But having dared to declare it: being Himself, He is all things. He does not acquire them from the outside. But He bestows them from within. And this is the sensible and intelligible glory of God: to pervade all things. <gloss>And</gloss> never is it merely of long duration. Since <gloss>in</gloss> having been left behind by His own—when <gloss>it is</gloss>—I mean of those things that exist, I mean of God. <gloss>For</gloss> of that which exists, nothing is outside.

<summary>This passage argues that God is not a detached or unthinking creator, but an omnipresent mind that pervades and sustains the entire cosmos through continuous activity. And nothing is outside of him, nor is he outside of anything. These things, O Asclepius, will seem true to you if you understand them, but to the ignorant, they will seem beyond belief. For to understand is to believe, and to disbelieve is to fail to understand. For my word <term>logos: here meaning speech or reasoned discourse</term> reaches as far as the truth. But the mind <term>nous: the faculty of spiritual intuition or higher intellect</term> is great, and being led by this word up to a certain point, it eventually reaches the truth. And having considered all things, and finding them in harmony with the things interpreted by the word, the mind believes; and in that beautiful faith, it finds its rest. Therefore, to those who have understood the things previously spoken by God, they are believable; but to those who have not understood, they are unbelievable. Let these things, then, be said concerning understanding.

Yesterday’s discourse, O Asclepius, I dedicated to you. But today’s, it is only right to dedicate to God. <meta>This page continues the Hermetic discourse from Libellus X, often titled "The Key." In the first <term>the Primal Being or God</term>, his activity <term>energeia: active power or operation</term>, or his will, or his essence <term>ousia: substance or being</term>, is to will <gloss>1st section</gloss> that all things exist. But the Good does not belong to living creatures simply because it is the cause of life; nor is the Good simply "living" in the animal sense. If this is the case, we are absolutely compelled to recognize that the Good exists by will, without which it is possible neither for anything to exist nor to come into being. 

The Father is the cause of children, of the seed, and of nourishment, having received the desire for the Good through the medium of life. For the Good is also the creative principle. And this, in turn, is not active in anyone except in him alone who, while receiving nothing, possesses all things and brings them into existence for the purpose of loving him. For the one who acts <unclear>or how</unclear> is subject to much time. Within time, one creates while another does not; or there is a distinction of quality or quantity. 

For by struggling, one creates a certain quality, but another does the opposite. But God and the Father and the Good consist in being all things. Thus, then, we are unable to see this. For he wills to be this, and he is; and he is most especially himself. And all things have come into being because of this. For it is the unique property of the Good to be known <note>original: "ginōskesthai" – a reference to Gnosis, or direct spiritual knowledge</note>. It is...

<meta>This page continues Libellus X (The Key) of the Corpus Hermeticum. Hermes Trismegistus is explaining to his disciple Tat the consequences of the soul's ignorance and the nature of "gnosis" (divine knowledge) as the only path to virtue and deification.</meta>

<unclear>to</unclear> these, the soul plunges itself like a reptile. This is the evil of the soul. And the vice of the soul is ignorance <note>original: "agnosia" — a state of being without knowledge, specifically regarding the divine</note>. For when a soul knows nothing of <unclear>the things that are</unclear>, nor the nature of God, nor the Good, it is blind and is violently tossed about by the passions of the body. The wretched soul, in its ignorance <margin>X</margin> of itself, serves strange and wicked bodies, carrying the body like a heavy burden. It does not rule, but is instead ruled; this is the vice of the soul.

The opposite, however, is the virtue of the soul: knowledge <term>Gnosis: in the Hermetic tradition, this is not just intellectual data, but a direct, experiential realization of God that transforms the seeker</term>. For he who knows is both good and pious, and is already divine. And who is <insert>such a man</insert>? He is one who does not speak much, nor listen to much. For he who occupies himself with two-fold arguments <gloss>O child</gloss>, fights with shadows in his battles. For God, <gloss>O child</gloss>, is neither proclaimed out loud nor is He heard through the ears. 

This being the case, the senses exist in all beings because they cannot <gloss>they</gloss> exist without Him. But knowledge differs greatly from sense-perception. For sense-perception occurs from what prevails over us <note>meaning the physical stimuli that dominate our attention</note>, but knowledge is the fulfillment of understanding <term>episteme: systematic knowledge or science, which Hermes here describes as a divine gift</term>.

Understanding is a gift of God. For all understanding is incorporeal, using the Mind <note>original: "Nous" — the highest faculty of the human soul, often translated as "Intellect" or "Intuition"</note> itself as its instrument, and the Mind in turn uses the body as its instrument. Both, therefore, enter into bodies—both the intelligible and the material. For all things must be composed of antithesis and opposition; it is impossible for it to be otherwise. Who then is this material god? It is the world <note>original: "kosmos" — here referring to the physical universe as a living, though material, deity</note>.

<summary>Hermes describes ignorance as the soul's greatest vice, leading to enslavement by the body, while divine knowledge (gnosis) leads to virtue and divinity. He distinguishes between physical sensation and the higher gift of understanding mediated by the Mind.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse from Libellus X, moving into the themes often found in Libellus XI of the Corpus Hermeticum. The text transitions from the nature of the soul's ignorance to a cosmic hierarchy involving God, the Aeon, the Cosmos, and Man.</meta>

God is indeed good. For He is not <gloss>being</gloss> material, so He <gloss>who</gloss> is not subject to passion <note>original: "empathes" — to be affected by external, physical, or emotional stimuli; God is viewed as beyond these influences</note>. The <gloss>Aeon</gloss> <note>original: "Aion" — often personified as a divine power representing eternity or the first stage of creation</note> is above <gloss>such</gloss> passions of all things. Second is the Aeon, and he is self-sufficient. And he himself is warlike <note>original: "polemodes" — though literally "warlike," this may refer to a state of dynamic tension or a scribal error for "manifold"</note> yet always existing. Being in the cosmos <note>original: "kosmos" — the ordered universe</note> and always becoming, he always moves upon the poles, and that which was before. He is mobile, for motion indeed exists. It is day. But the solitary one <unclear>of these things</unclear> moves with a material motion in the same place. Since the cosmos is spherical, it has no head. Nor would there be anything material above it, just as there are no feet, nor a base below. For all is Mind <term>Nous: the divine intellect or higher consciousness that governs the universe</term>. And Mind is the head, moving itself in a spherical manner, for those things of the head. God says motion is in the region of the head. From this, the soul <gloss>not</gloss> is immortal <note>The interlinear gloss "ou" (not) suggests a correction or nuance to the word "immortal," perhaps "not-mortal" or "not-dying"</note> by nature. Just as the soul is made for the body, and having <gloss>more</gloss> soul than the form of the body. Wherefore from afar the Sun is that which holds the greater part of the strength for the whole body of every living being. Just as the whole consists of both the immaterial and the intelligible <note>original: "noetou" — things perceived by the mind rather than the physical senses</note>. The cosmos is first; man is the second living being. After the cosmos, but the first among mortals. Among the living beings, he possesses that which has a soul. But he is no longer merely "not good," but even evil because he is mortal. For the cosmos is not "good" because it is mobile; but it is not "good" <note>The context suggests the scribe likely meant "not evil"</note> because it is immortal. But man is evil both because he is <gloss>mobile</gloss> and because he is mortal. And the soul of man is carried by this cosmos.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical instruction of the Corpus Hermeticum, specifically expanding on the physiological and metaphysical relationship between God, the Cosmos, and Man. It follows the previous discussion on the nature of Mind and the spheres.</meta>

The Mind <term>Nous: the divine intellect or higher consciousness</term> is in the word <note>original: "logos" — can mean speech, reason, or the structural principle of the universe</note>. The word is in the soul. The soul is in the body.
The spirit <term>Pneuma: a vital "breath" or life-force thought to carry the soul's commands through the body</term> permeates through the veins, the arteries, and the blood,
and moves the living being. It is as if it carries the being. For this reason,
some have even believed the soul to be blood. They were mistaken,
not knowing its nature; they did not know that first the spirit must withdraw into
the soul, and only then does the blood congeal, and the veins and arte-
ries become emptied. Then, in this way, the living being passes away. And this is
the death of the body. All things depend on one principle <note>original: "arche" — the source, origin, or beginning of all things</note>.
And the principle comes from the One and Only. The principle moves,
so that it may again become a principle. But the One alone stands still;
it is not moved. And all other things follow this way. The cosmos is from God, and exists within God. And
man is in the cosmos; and God possesses <note>or "contains"</note> it. The "Whole" is the cosmos.
And the cosmos becomes the son of God, and man is like a grandson
of the cosmos. For God is not ignorant of man; rather, He
knows him fully and wishes to be known. This alone is salvation
for man: the knowledge <term>Gnosis: experiential, spiritual knowledge of the divine</term> of God. This is the ascent
to Olympus <note>original: "Olympion" — used here to represent the divine realm or the heavens rather than the physical mountain</note>. This belongs only to the good soul. Yet a soul is never good
if it is being dragged down or if it exists by necessity <note>i.e., being forced into the material world against its divine nature</note>. How then do you say,
O Trismegistus <note>referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary sage</note>, that you have seen the soul of a child? Because they are new,
and [the soul] has not yet admitted its diffusion <note>original: "diachysin" — an expansion or scattering of the soul's focus into the physical sensations of the body</note> with its body.

It emphasizes that the only path to salvation and the divine "Olympus" is the direct knowledge (gnosis) of God.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "Key" of Hermes Trismegistus, a central discourse in the Corpus Hermeticum. It picks up from the discussion on how the Mind (Nous) relates to the lower faculties of the human being and the process of the soul's withdrawal from the physical body upon death.</meta>

The composition of these garments, O child, occurs in a body of earth. For it is impossible for the Mind to seat itself naked and alone within an earthly body. For neither can the earthly [body]...

<meta>This page continues the "Key" of Hermes Trismegistus from the Corpus Hermeticum (Book X). It transitions from discussing the Mind's fiery "tunic" to the moral consequences for the soul. <meta>This page continues the discourse between the Divine Mind (Nous) and Hermes Trismegistus from the eleventh book of the Corpus Hermeticum. Therefore, as a sign of this, idleness <note>original: *argia*, meaning inactivity or laziness</note> is nowhere in the world, nor even in God. For "idleness" is an empty name, both regarding the one who makes and that which is being made. It is necessary for all things to be coming into being, always, and in every place. For the maker is in all things, not established in any one particular place, nor acting in only one specific thing, but acting in all things. For since he is an **active power**, he is not self-contained apart from the things that come to be; rather, all things are his "becoming." 

Behold through me the world <term>Kosmos: the universe conceived as an ordered and beautiful system</term> lying before your sight, and contemplate its beauty with precision. It is a body that is undefiled, and nothing is more ancient than it; yet through all time, it remains in its prime and young—and indeed, more than in its prime. 

See also the seven worlds <note>original: *hepta kosmoi*, referring to the seven planetary spheres: Moon, Mercury, Venus, Sun, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note> laid out beneath, adorned in **eternal order** <term>Aionio taxei: the fixed, everlasting arrangement of the stars and planets</term>, and filling up eternity in their different courses. 

And all things are full of light, but destructive fire <note>The text suggests that the celestial fire is a life-giving light rather than a consuming flame</note> is nowhere. For the friendship and the blending of opposites and unlikes has become a shining light, illuminated by the activity of God, who is the parent of all good, and the ruler and leader of every order of the seven worlds. 

And look at the moon <note>original: *selene*</note>, the precursor to those spheres, the instrument of nature for all things, transforming the matter below. 

And the earth is established in the middle of the whole as the sediment <note>original: *hypostathmen*, meaning dregs or the solid matter that settles at the bottom of a liquid—reflecting the ancient view that the heavy, material earth sits at the center of the lighter celestial spheres</note> of the beautiful world, the nourisher and nurse of all things upon the earth.

# Libellus XI: The Mind to Hermes

And one must behold <gloss>to behold</gloss> the multitude of **immortal beings** <gloss>of the immortals</gloss> and of the mortal beings <gloss>living beings</gloss>, starting from the central hearth <note>original: *hestia*; refers to the center of the universe or the source of life</note> <gloss>hand</gloss>. And between both of them—the immortals and the mortals—the Moon travels her circuit. She fills all things with soul <term>Psyche: the life-giving principle or breath that animates a body</term>, and she moves all things. Some she moves around the heavens, and others around the earth. 

And neither do the things on the right move toward the left, nor the things on the left toward the right; nor do the things above move below, nor those below move above. And know that all this creation, O my son <note>The text uses "child," a traditional way for a teacher to address a devoted student</note> Tat, is not without a share of life and sensation. For it is uniform; it possesses a soul and it moves. 

But it is impossible for these things to be brought together into one without a Unifier <note>original: *synagontos*; the one who gathers or brings things together into a coherent whole</note>. It is truly necessary that there be someone, and that he be entirely one. For where there are differences—since the movements are many and the bodies are not alike—yet a single speed is ordered through them all, it is impossible for there to be two or more makers. For a **single order** is maintained over many things. 

If there were many creators, jealousy toward the one who is superior would follow among the many, and they would quarrel. And if the maker of the changeable and mortal beings were different from the maker of the immortals, the maker of mortals would have desired to make immortals too, just as the maker of the immortals would have desired to make mortals. 

Consider this: even if there were two makers, since there is only one matter <term>Hyle: the raw, underlying substance of the universe</term> and one soul, which of them would be the provider for the creation? And if the power of creation belongs to both, with whom...

ητ

<summary>In this passage, the Divine Mind explains the role of the Moon as a mediator between immortal and mortal realms and argues that the perfect, synchronized movement of the cosmos proves it was created by a single, unified God rather than multiple conflicting deities.</summary>
<keywords>Moon, immortality, mortality, Tat, Hermes, Psyche, Hyle, monotheism, celestial order, divine unity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse between the Divine Mind (Nous) and Hermes Trismegistus. The previous page discussed how a single Orderer is necessary for the cosmos, concluding with the argument that if there were many creators, the universe would fall into chaos.</meta>

...the greater part. But let it be understood: this does not apply to every living body, but to those having their constitution from **matter** and **soul**—both the immortal, and the mortal, and the irrational. For every body, being alive, is ensouled. But that which is not alive is simply **matter** <term>Hyle: the raw, physical substance of the universe which requires a soul to become active</term> once again by itself. 

And the **soul** <term>Psyche: the animating principle or "breath" that gives a body life and consciousness</term> likewise, by itself, being present with the Creator, is the cause of life. And of all life, the cause is He who is immortal. How then could mortal living beings be the works of a mortal maker? And how could there be things that are immortal and produce immortality, if He were not the maker of living beings?

And that there is clearly someone who makes these things, and that He is one, is evident. For there is one soul, and one life, and one matter. Who then is this? Who else could it be but the one God? For to whom would it be fitting to make ensouled beings, if not to God alone? 

Therefore, you will admit it is most ridiculous and inconsistent <note>original: *anarmosteron*; literally "out of harmony" or "unfitting" for the nature of the divine</note> to attribute this to any but Him. To say that the sun and God hold one and the same rank of place is also absurd. He Himself, therefore, makes all things. It is much more ridiculous—and yet a great task for God—to say He produces life and soul and immortality and change, but does not also produce the body. 

For you see, and you speak, and you hear, and you smell <note>original: *osphratai*; the Greek verb here shifts to the third person "he smells," though it refers to the human sensory experience</note>, and you touch, and you walk, and you think, and you breathe. And it is not one who sees, and another who hears, and another who speaks. For the same...

<summary>The Mind argues that a single, immortal God is the source of all life, soul, and matter, rejecting the idea of multiple creators. He explains that the unity of human senses—the fact that the same "you" sees, hears, and thinks—proves the unity of the Creator who fashioned both the spirit and the body.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discourse where the Divine Mind (Nous) explains to Hermes Trismegistus that God is not a distant creator but is intimately present in every sensation and biological function. ...simplicity. But God is the one who perceives through smell; and God is the one concerned with the heartbeat. And God is the one who thinks; and God is the one who breathes—existing in that which is the All. But not even those things can exist apart from God; for just as [if life were] taken away from [a body], it would no longer be a living creature, so too if God were taken away—which is not lawful <note>original: *themis*; a term for divine law or what is fundamentally right and sanctioned by the gods</note> to even say—it would no longer be God.

But it has been demonstrated that nothing can exist [without] the name of God. And why is it as I have said, unless it is not lawful to say what God truly is? If He is not idle but is perfect, then He creates all <gloss>things</gloss>. Toward what I am saying, give me your full attention, O Hermes: it is easy to understand the work of God. It is one thing: so that all things may come to be. And of the things that come to be—whether they have happened once or are about to <gloss>happen</gloss>—this, O dearest one, is **life**. And this is the **Beautiful**; and this is the **Good**. 

This is **Mind** <term>Nous: the divine intellect or cosmic consciousness that organizes the universe</term>. And if He <unclear>makes</unclear> Mind His work, see also how the things that <gloss>come to be</gloss> are born whenever He wills it, as a sign of that God who is like them. He, then, is not idle; for He does not depart from Himself. Being a self-worker, He is always within the work, being Himself that which He makes.

But if He were to be separated from it, all things would necessarily collapse and all things would die, as if life no longer existed. But if all <gloss>things</gloss> are living creatures and life is one, then God also is one. And to say it again: living creatures are both those in heaven and those on earth.

<meta>This page continues the revelation from the Divine Mind (Nous) to Hermes. The previous section established that God is the animator of all physical processes and that nothing exists outside of His influence. This page explores the nature of life and death, defined not as creation and destruction, but as the joining and dissolving of eternal elements within a hierarchical chain of being.</meta>

...not a single living creature exists by chance, but rather <gloss>instead</gloss> <gloss>#</gloss> possesses its own being <insert>to have</insert> <gloss>#</gloss> through the agency of God who is present in all things. **Life** is the union of Mind <term>Nous: the divine intellect or cosmic consciousness</term> and soul; but **death** is not the destruction of those elements which were joined together, but rather the dissolution of that union.

Thus, God is the source of the image of Eternity <term>Aion: the eternal age or timeless duration</term>; Eternity is the image of the Cosmos <term>the ordered universe</term>; the Cosmos is the image of the Sun; and the Sun is the image of Humanity.

They call this transformation "death" because the visible form is dissolved, while life itself moves into the unseen realm. Regarding this dissolution, O Hermes <insert>in</insert>—my dearest friend in this discourse—I tell you that the Cosmos also <unclear>demonstrates</unclear> <insert>x</insert> this change, as you hear; for a part of it undergoes this every day, yet it remains within the unseen realm, never truly being destroyed. 

For if the Cosmos experiences these conditions—both movements and concealments—then movement is "becoming" <note>original: *genesis*; the process of coming into existence or birth</note>, while concealment is "renewal." The Cosmos is forever possessing form <gloss>as</gloss>; it does not have forms imposed upon it from the outside, but it is its own transformation within itself. Since the Cosmos is eternally formed, the Father <unclear>is</unclear> the One who is All-Eternal, and He could never be formless. 

If He were also "all-formed" <note>original: *pantomorphos*; having every possible shape or form</note>, He would be identical to the Cosmos. But if He possessed only a single form, He would be lesser than the Cosmos in that respect. What then shall we say He is? Let us not allow our discourse to fall into confusion <note>original: *aporia*; a state of philosophical exhaustion or a dead-end</note>; for while it is difficult to grasp the nature of God through thought, He must possess one [form]...

### + On Mind to Tat +

<margin>12</margin>
Mind <note>original: *nous*</note>, O Tat <note>The son and disciple of Hermes Trismegistus</note>, is from the very essence of God; if indeed there

But in irrational animals, the soul is a life empty of Mind.

stretching them toward desires into which they are carried by the rush 
of appetite, straining toward the irrational. And just like

irrationally desire, and they do not stop. Nor do they master their evils.

<meta>This page continues Libellus XII of the Corpus Hermeticum. If the Mind and the Law can restrain our passions, then we must have some degree of choice, which seems to contradict the idea that everything is fated.</meta>

<gloss>1</gloss> Angers and <gloss>2</gloss> the <gloss>2</gloss> desires <gloss>1</gloss> resulting from <gloss>2</gloss> vice reach extremes. Against these, as if by hunger and scrutiny, stands the practice <gloss>of the</gloss> Law. At this point, the argument concerning fate, which was previously stated <unclear>absolutely</unclear>, risks being overturned. For if it was altogether fated for a certain person to commit adultery or sacrilege <note>original: *hierosylēsai*, specifically referring to the crime of robbing a temple or committing an act of profound disrespect against the divine</note>, it is clear that the one who acted is punished unjustly—since they committed the deed by the necessity of fate. For it <unclear>was said</unclear> that all deeds are the works of fate. 

If one holds to that view, nothing is sinful <note>original: *hamartikon*, meaning an error, a "missing of the mark," or a moral failing</note>. Neither good nor evil would happen to us by our own choice—rather, it was fated that we should do evil to all. And for this reason, one acts: so that the sufferer may endure what he did <note>This refers to the law of retribution or "eye for an eye," suggesting that if fate rules all, then even the punishment is just a fated cycle of suffering without moral weight.</note>. 

But the current argument regarding evil and fate is unlike what I have said before; now our discourse is about what is in our power <term>eph' hēmin: a technical term in ancient philosophy for human agency or free will—that which is "up to us" despite external circumstances</term>. What then of the ancients? And how does this agency exist in different beings? Among these dissimilar humans, [it is present], but in the irrational animals, there is no bridle. On the other hand, in irrational animals, it is the beneficial force [of nature]. 

But Law is in all things. It extinguishes both the spirited part <term>thymikon: the seat of the soul where anger, pride, and the "fire" of the personality reside</term> and the desiring part <term>epithymetikon: the part of the soul responsible for physical appetites and bodily lusts</term>. And so, for men of renown surely [the Law acts as a guide]. But if men are irrational, they are all the same, subject to fate.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue of Libellus XII from the Corpus Hermeticum. The previous section established that while all humans are subject to Fate (Heimarmene), those who possess the Mind (Nous) are not "evil" in their suffering. This page deepens the investigation into the Mind's absolute authority over destiny and begins a technical discussion on how the Mind interacts with the "passions" or "impulses" of the physical body.</meta>

It is quite clear, indeed, that you have raised this inquiry with an excess of zeal and logic; yet this specific point was not included in my previous discourse. I am speaking now about the relationship between Fate <note>original: *heimarmene*, the inescapable web of cause and effect in the material world</note> and the Mind. 

For if one examines these arguments accurately, my child, you will find that the Mind is truly the absolute master over all things—over Fate and Law and everything else. Nothing is impossible for it: neither placing a human soul above Fate, nor [guiding] a soul that has been negligent and happens to fall under the power of Fate. Let this much be said on that matter. 

The teachings of the Good Spirit <note>original: *Agathos Daimon*, the personification of divine wisdom often cited as a primary source for Hermes</note> are excellent and divine, just as are his other works and benefits. But clarify this one thing further for me. For you were saying that in irrational animals, the Mind does not operate as a moral judge, but rather works in cooperation with their impulses. 

Now, the impulses <note>original: *hormai*, the natural drives or instincts that move a living being toward action</note> of irrational animals are, I believe, passions <note>original: *pathe*, here meaning "afflictions" or "sensations" that a body undergoes</note>. If the Mind cooperates with these impulses, and these impulses are passions, then it follows that the Mind itself must be a passion, taking on the color and character of the passions themselves.

Well said, my child! Your inquiry is noble, and it is only right that I provide an answer. 

My child, all incorporeal things <note>original: *asomata*, things lacking physical substance or weight</note> that reside within a body are subject to passion; indeed, in a strict sense, they are themselves "passions." For everything that initiates motion is incorporeal, while everything that is set in motion is a body.

And even the <term>incorporeal: *asomaton*, that which exists without a physical body</term> is moved by the Mind. But motion is a <term>passion: *pathos*, meaning a state of being acted upon or undergoing a change</term>.

And there is nothing in this [cosmos] throughout the whole of <term>Eternity: *aion*, referring here to the infinite span of the divine world</term>—neither of the whole nor of the parts—which does not live. For not even one thing is dead; it neither has been, nor is. For it is not necessary for anything in a living cosmos, or its parts, to be [dead].

Therefore, life is also a necessity. How then can the parts in the being of the whole, and in the image [of God], and in the fullness of life, be dead? For "deadness" is decay, and decay is destruction. How then can any part of the **imperishable** be decayed, or anything of God be destroyed? 

The cosmos does not die, even if the living beings within it—being its parts—[appear to do so]. The word "death" is a <note>original: *planômenos*, meaning wandering or misleading</note> misleading name for a result...

<summary>This page argues for the inherent divinity and immortality of the cosmos, claiming that because the universe is the 'Fullness of Life' and an image of God, nothing within it can truly die or be destroyed.</summary>
<keywords>Cosmos as God, Pleroma, immortality, Aeon, Monads, imperishability, nature of death</keywords>

--- Page 226 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Hermes and his son Tat regarding the nature of the universe. The previous page established the cosmos as a living, divine entity where death is impossible; this page elaborates on how "death" is actually just a process of change and movement within a living system.</meta>

-> <image-desc>Decorative flourish or reference mark at the top center of the page.</image-desc> <-

...die? What happens <unclear>is not that</unclear> things die, my
child; rather, as composite bodies they are dissolved. This
dissolution is not death, but the dissolution of a <term>Mixture: *krasis*, the blending of elements that form a body</term>. Things are
dissolved not so that they might be destroyed, but so that they may become new. Since
the active power of life is motion, what then in the world
is motionless? Nothing, my child.
<margin>X</margin> "Does even the earth seem motionless to you, Father?" No, child; it is also
moved in many ways, and yet it alone is also stable. How could it not be ridiculous for
the most visible part to be motionless when the Mother of all is in such motion? For
it is impossible for anything to be born without motion. The most obvious
fact of all is this: if any part is lesser, it will be idle; and I say that
"motionless" means nothing other than idleness.
Everything in the world, therefore, is always in motion, my child. And that which moves
in the world moves either by decreasing or increasing. And that which moves
also lives. And is it not necessary that every living thing be the same? For while
the cosmos as a whole is **unchangeable**, all of its 
parts are subject to change. But nothing is perishable 
or truly destroyed. It is only names that confuse 
human beings. For <term>Becoming: *genesis*, the process of coming into existence</term> is not the start of life, but the start of perception; nor is 
<margin>X</margin> "change" death, but a <term>Release: *lysis*, an unbinding or loosening of the soul from the body</term>. This is the state of every living thing.

<summary>This passage from the Hermetic corpus redefines death as 'dissolution' rather than destruction, arguing that because the entire cosmos is a living, moving entity, nothing within it can truly cease to exist.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, dissolution, motion, cosmos, change, perception, immortality of the soul</keywords>

...death <note>original: "θάνατος ποτέ," implying death never occurs in the sense of total destruction</note> never exists; but life is spirit, nature, and the Mind, from which every living thing is composed. Every living thing, therefore, is immortal, but above all is <term>Man: *anthrōpos*, here abbreviated as ἄνος in the sacred style</term>, for he is capable of receiving <term>God: *theos*, here abbreviated as θῦ</term> and shares in His very essence. For to this living being alone does <term>God: θς</term> speak:
<margin>x</margin> by night through dreams, and by day through signs <note>original: "συμβόλων," referring to omens or symbolic events</note>.
<margin>x</margin> And through all things He foretells the future to him: through birds, through entrails, through inspiration, and through the sacred oak.
<margin>x</margin> Wherefore <term>Man: ἄνος</term> has the power to understand all things—those that have passed, those present, and those yet to come. And consider this too, my child: that each living thing dwells in its own specific part of the cosmos—the aquatic creatures in the water, the land-dwellers on the earth, and those of the heights in the air. But <term>Man: ἄνος</term> makes use of all these: earth, water, air, and fire. He beholds the heavens and even touches them with his perception. But <term>God: θς</term> is both around all things and through all things; for He is Activity and Power. And it is not at all difficult to understand <term>God: θν</term>, my child. If you wish to behold Him, look at the arrangement of the cosmos and the beautiful order of that arrangement.
<margin>x</margin> See the necessity of all that appears, and the <term>Providence: *pronoia*, divine foresight</term> of things that have been and things that are becoming.
<margin>x</margin> See how matter itself is filled to the brim with life. See how great a <term>God: θν</term> this is, moving in harmony with all good things, and with the gods, and with the <term>Daemons: *daimones*, divine intermediaries</term>.

<summary>Hermes explains that Man is unique among living beings for his ability to communicate with God and inhabit all realms of the physical world. <meta>This page continues the discourse in Libellus XI of the Corpus Hermeticum, where the Divine Mind (Nous) explains the nature of God's relationship to the universe. Following the discussion of Man’s unique perception, the text moves to a more metaphysical level, arguing that all aspects of existence—from necessity to matter itself—are actually "activities" or manifestations of God. It concludes that God is the "All," encompassing and transcending all physical properties.</meta>

<gloss>And necessity; it is clear that just as the activities are...</gloss>
And necessity <note>original: "ἀνάγκη" (anankē), often personified as a cosmic force that governs the laws of nature</note>. It is clear that just as the activities exist,
nothing is energized <note>original: "ἐνεργεῖται," meaning to be put into action or manifestation</note> except by some agent. Otherwise they would seem
lifeless. But He who is the Cosmos, and the Father, and the
Eternity <note>original: "αἰῶνά" (aiōna), the personification of limitless time or the divine realm</note>, is neither [simply] life nor immortality. And indeed,
there is Necessity, and Providence, and Nature, and Soul, and Mind.
And the permanence of all these things is what is called the Good. And
[the Good is the source] of those things now coming into being, or those that have already happened. Where
could one find the strength of these past events, except through the First Principle? Not without
being apart from God; for what place of His could you set aside? How
then could she <note>referring to Nature or the Soul mentioned previously</note> be idle and not be acted upon? For if she 
acts, she is acted upon by someone. For we have said that activities 
are parts of God. Or, by someone then all things are given life—all 
living beings. Or, by someone the immortals are made immortal. By someone 
the changeable things are changed. Whether you speak of matter, or 
body, or essence <note>original: "οὐσίαν" (ousian), the fundamental substance of a thing</note>, know that these themselves are activities 
of God. And <term>Matter is the activity of materiality</term>. And the 
bodies are the activity of embodiment. And essence is the activity of essentiality. And this is God: 
the All. And in the All there is nothing which is not. God 
is neither magnitude, nor place, nor quality, nor shape, 
nor is He able to be contained by time. For He is all things. And the All...

<summary>This page argues that every force in the universe, including matter and necessity, is an 'activity' of God. It concludes with a panentheistic vision where God is both the totality of all things and a being who transcends physical limitations like size and time.</summary>

### Hermes Trismegistus to his son Tat: On Rebirth, and on the Promise of Silence : +

<margin>7</margin>
<margin>3</margin>
<margin>in</margin>
In the general discourses, O father, you spoke in riddles and not clearly <note>original: "τηλαυγῶς" (tēlaugōs), meaning "with far-shining clarity"</note> <margin>6</margin> when you conversed <gloss>as</gloss> about divinity <gloss>os</gloss>. And you did not reveal it // by saying that no one can be saved before rebirth <note>original: "παλιγγενεσίας" (palingenesias), the spiritual process of being "born again" into a divine state</note>. And when I became a suppliant upon the descent from the mountain, after you had spoken with me, I inquired to learn <gloss>to learn</gloss> the teaching of rebirth, because this is the only thing of all that I do not know. And you said that when you were about to be estranged from the world, you would hand it down to me. I have made myself ready, and I have strengthened <note>original: "ἀπηνδρείωσα" (apēndreiōsa), literally "to make manly," meaning to fortify the will</note> my own mind against the deception of the world. But fulfill for me those things that are lacking, by which you said you would hand down <gloss>os</gloss> [the teaching] of rebirth, whether in <unclear>speech</unclear> or in secret proclamation. I do not know, O Thrice-Greatest, from what kind of womb a person is born, or from what kind of seed? 

O child, [the womb is] Intellectual Wisdom in silence. And the seed is the True Good. 

By whose sowing, O father? For I am altogether at a loss. 

By the will of God, O child.

<summary>This page marks the beginning of a key Hermetic initiation where Tat asks Hermes to explain the mystery of spiritual rebirth, having successfully detached himself from worldly illusions. Hermes reveals that this rebirth is not physical, but occurs through the "womb" of Wisdom and the "seed" of the Good, sown by the Will of God.</summary>

<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Tat, Rebirth, Palingenesia, Intellectual Wisdom, Divine Will, Silence, Initiation, Corpus Hermeticum</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Libellus XIII of the Corpus Hermeticum. In the preceding text, the disciple Tat requested that his father, Hermes Trismegistus, fulfill his promise to teach the secret of "rebirth" (palingenesia) now that Tat has prepared his mind by rejecting the deceptions of the world.</meta>

And once [I asked], <gloss>how much</gloss> [of] the general discourse, O father <note>original: "πε͂ρ" (per), a scribal abbreviation for "πάτερ" (pater)</note>? <gloss>For</gloss> [lest] I be <gloss>the</gloss> [one] who is without a share <gloss>of</gloss> <gloss>the whole</gloss> of my essence and of the stain of forgetfulness. Truly, as with the general discourse which concerns the All.

The All is in all, composed of all powers.

You speak in riddles to me, O father, and you do not speak as a father to a son.

This race <note>original: "γένος" (genos), referring to the spiritual lineage or "kind" of those who are reborn</note>, O child, is not taught; but when He wills, it is brought to remembrance by God <note>original: "θῦ" (thū), a scribal abbreviation for "θεοῦ" (theou)</note>.

You tell me impossible things, O father, and things forced [upon my mind]. Therefore, I wish to reply rightly to these things. I have become a stranger-son to my father’s kind. Do not begrudge me, O father; I am a legitimate son. Explain to me the difference in yourself and the birth from God.

What shall I say, child? I have nothing to say. Except this: seeing within myself an unformed vision having come to be by the mercy of God, I have gone out of myself into an immortal body, and I am now not who I was before, but I have been born in Mind <note>original: "νῷ" (nō), the divine intellect or spiritual consciousness</note>. 

This matter is not taught, nor is it perceived by this material <note>original: "πλαστῷ" (plastō), meaning molded or fabricated</note> element, because of which I have left behind my first molded, composite form. I have been broken away [from that], and though I have measure [in the physical sense], I am now of God. 

You see me, O child, with your eyes; you understand what I am by gazing with your body and your sight. But I am not perceived with these eyes now, O child.

You have cast me into no small madness and an ecstasy of the mind, O father!

<summary>In this pivotal dialogue, Hermes explains to Tat that spiritual rebirth cannot be taught but is granted by God's mercy, resulting in a transformation where the sage "leaves himself" to inhabit an immortal body of Mind.</summary>
<keywords>rebirth, palingenesia, divine mercy, immortal body, spiritual vision, Hermes Trismegistus, Tat, gnosis</keywords>

--- Page 231 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue of Libellus XIII from the Corpus Hermeticum. The disciple Tat is reacting to the revelation of his father, Hermes Trismegistus, who has just described his own spiritual rebirth into a divine, immaterial body. The catchword '9' at the bottom suggests this is part of a numbered sequence of pages or quires.</meta>

But I do not see my [former] self now. I wish, O child, that you too had gone out of yourself, as if dreaming in sleep—though I would say this is without [actual] sleep—but then you might laugh at this babbling <note>original: "γλωσσαλγίας" (glossalgias), literally 'tongue-pain', meaning excessive or idle talk</note>. But let that which is happening to you be by the will of God.

I understand now, O father; you have led me into a state of speechlessness <term>aphasia: a silence where one is unable to express the divine in words</term>, for I have been left behind by my former wits. For I see the same magnitude [as before], yet as if in a reflected image, and in this there is falsehood. For the mortal form changes day by day; in time it turns toward growth or decay, being a falsehood.

What then is true, O Thrice-Greatest?

That which is not clouded, O child. That which is not bounded <note>original: "διοριζόμενον" (diorizomenon), meaning limited or defined by borders</note>. The colorless. The formless. The immutable. The naked. The shining. That which is comprehensible to itself alone. The unchangeable Good. The bodiless.

I truly remember, just as it seemed <gloss>to me</gloss> while these things were being spoken by you <gloss>to my mind</gloss>; but my senses have been sealed up before this <gloss>magnitude of the</gloss> thought.

So it is, O child. That which is carried upward like fire, and downward like earth; and the fluid like water, and the breathing-together like air <note>Hermes refers here to the four classical elements that make up the material world</note>. How will you understand this with your senses—that which is not hard, not wet, not bound tight, not penetrable? It is understood only by power <term>dynamis: the inherent potential or spiritual force</term> and activity <term>energeia: the actualization of that divine force</term>, yet it requires one who is capable [of such understanding].

<summary>Hermes describes the nature of the divine, "true" reality as formless and immutable, contrasting it with the changing material world. Tat experiences a spiritual silence (aphasia) as his physical senses are overwhelmed by this higher mental magnitude.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Tat, rebirth, aphasia, four elements, truth vs. falsehood, divine attributes, dynamis, energeia</keywords>

--- Page 232 ---
<meta>This page continues Libellus XIII of the Corpus Hermeticum, a dialogue on rebirth (palingenesia) between the sage Hermes Trismegistus and his son/disciple Tat. Having established that the divine cannot be perceived with physical senses, Hermes now instructs Tat on the necessity of internal purification.</meta>

...to understand it in its generation. I am therefore incapable, O father.

May it not be so, O child! Rather, draw it into yourself and it will come. Will it, and it comes to pass. The purification of the body is the setting aside <note>original: "θήσεις" (theses), likely a corruption of "ἀπόθεσις" meaning putting away or discarding</note> of the senses. For it becomes an end. If the [mercy] of the divinity spoke to us, we would be purged from the irrational torments of matter. 

And do I have tormentors within myself, O father?

Not a few, O child, but many and fearful ones. 

The first torment, O child, is ignorance. 
The second, grief. 
The third, lack of self-control. 
The fourth, desire. 
The fifth, injustice. 
The sixth, greed. 
The seventh, deceit. 
The eighth, envy. 
The ninth, guile. 
The tenth, anger. 
The eleventh, rashness. 
The twelfth, malice.

These are twelve in number; and under these, O child, are many others. Through the prison-house of the body, they force the inner man <term>the essential, spiritual self distinct from the physical form</term> to suffer through the senses. But they depart from the soul, not all at once, but as mercy is shown by God. And thus is established the method and the logic of rebirth <term>palingenesia: the spiritual process of being born again into a divine state</term>. 

Therefore, be silent, O child, and keep a holy silence <note>original: "εὐφήμησον" (euphemēson), a ritual command to maintain a sacred, prayerful silence during mysteries</note>. For this reason, the mercy from God toward us shall not cease <note>105</note>. Rejoice then, O child, as you are being purified by the powers of God for the articulation of the Word <note>original: "λόγου" (Logou), referring here to divine Reason or the Word of God</note>.

<summary>Hermes identifies twelve "torments" or vices rooted in the physical body that must be purged for a person to experience spiritual rebirth. He explains that through God's mercy, these vices depart, allowing the "inner man" to be restored.</summary>
<keywords>twelve torments, purification, rebirth, palingenesia, inner man, ignorance, divine mercy, Corpus Hermeticum</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from Libellus XIII of the Corpus Hermeticum. Hermes is in the process of invoking the ten divine "powers" (virtues) to displace the twelve "torments" (vices) he listed on the previous page, thereby effecting Tat’s spiritual rebirth.</meta>

...and now comes within us the knowledge of God. When this arrives, O child, ignorance is driven out. The knowledge of joy has come to us; and upon its arrival, O child, grief will flee to those who still give it a place <note>The text suggests that once divine joy enters, grief can only exist in those who have not yet undergone this transformation</note>. 

After joy, I summon the power of self-control—O sweetest power! Let us welcome it gladly, my child. See how, the moment it appeared, it pushed away lack of self-control. 

And now I summon the fourth: endurance, the power that stands against desire. This step, O child, is the very foundation of justice; for see how, without any external judgment, it has driven out injustice. We have been made just, O child, now that injustice is gone. 

As the sixth power, I summon fellowship <gloss>honors</gloss>, which stands against greed. Once <unclear>that vice</unclear> has departed, I summon truth. Deceit flees, and truth arrives. 

See how the Good is fulfilled, O child! Upon the arrival of truth, envy has departed from us; for with truth, the Good has also come to pass, together with life and light. And no longer shall any punishment of darkness <note>original: "τιμωρία" (timōria), referring back to the twelve torments/vices described as jailers of the soul</note> come upon us again. Instead, they have flown away, defeated in a rushing whirlwind. 

Now you have come to know, O child, the way of rebirth <term>palingenesia: the spiritual process of being born again into a divine state</term>. With the arrival of the Ten <note>The "Decad" or group of ten virtues that Hermes has been listing</note>, O child, the spiritual birth is complete; it is this birth which drives out the Twelve <note>The "Dodecad" of vices or torments listed on the preceding page</note>, and by this birth, we have been made divine <note>original: "ἐθεωρήθημεν" (etheōrēthēmen), suggesting that through this birth, the soul becomes a spectator of—and participant in—the divine nature</note>. 

Therefore, whoever has attained, through the mercy of...

<meta>This page continues the secret discourse of Hermes Trismegistus to his son Tat. Having just invoked the ten divine virtues to drive out the twelve "torments" or vices, Hermes now describes the ecstatic state of the initiate who has transcended physical limits and explains the numerological mystery of why ten powers can overcome twelve vices.</meta>

In knowl<gloss>edge</gloss> <gloss>within him</gloss>, having left behind bodily sensation, he comes to know himself. From these things he is composed <gloss>as a son</gloss> and rejoices, becoming unwavering through <gloss>the</gloss> God. Just as I envision, it is not with the sight of eyes, but by the intellectual activity through divine power. <unclear>I am alone</unclear>. I am in the earth. In the water. In the air. I am in animals. In plants. In the womb, before the womb, and after the womb. I am everywhere.

But tell me this further: how are the torments of darkness twelve in number, yet <gloss>by</gloss> ten powers they are pushed away? What is the manner of this, O Thrice-Greatest? This tabernacle <note>original: "σκῆνος" (skēnos), a term often used in Hermetic and Platonic texts to describe the physical body as a temporary "tent" for the soul</note>... And Oh, child, it has passed out <unclear>from there</unclear> from the life-bearing circle <note>The "life-bearing circle" refers to the Zodiac, which in Hermetic thought governs the physical body and the twelve vices/torments</note>...

And thus it is composed of the number twelve; though they are numbers, they represent the corruption of a single, multiform idea into the wandering of the <note>mortal/man?</note>; they are yoked within it, O child, united in their action. Rashness is inseparable from anger, and they are also indistinct from one another. It is likely then, according to right reason, that they make their departure just as <unclear>the renewal</unclear> <unclear>that is</unclear> from the ten powers or the Decad <note>The "Decad" (group of ten) represents divine perfection and the harmony that restores the soul</note> arrives. For the Decad, O child, is the begetter of souls <term>psychogonos: a term for a principle that generates or gives birth to the soul</term>. Life and Light are united there. And within it, the number of the Ennead <note>The "Ennead" refers to the number nine, often representing the sphere of the fixed stars just below the divine unity</note> is naturally <gloss>the</gloss> spirit's. Thus, for us, according to reason, the Decad contains the Ennead, and the Ennead contains the Decad.

<summary>Hermes describes the initiate's experience of omnipresence and explains how the ten divine virtues (the Decad) displace the twelve zodiacal vices to complete the spiritual rebirth.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discourse between Hermes Trismegistus and his son Tat. ...beyond which I see all, having gone out from myself: this is **rebirth** <term>palingenesia; from the Greek 'palin' (again) and 'genesis' (birth)</term>, O child. I have recorded this discourse concerning rebirth so that we might not be slanderers <note>original: διάβολοι (diaboloi), meaning those who falsely accuse or reveal secrets to the unworthy</note> of the All to the many, into whom God himself has entered.

Immortal knowledge <gloss>knowledges</gloss> reveals that you are born a god and a child of the One.

We hymn <gloss>u</gloss> <insert>and</insert> we praise; listen to the Powers. You do well to hasten to dissolve the tabernacle <note>the physical body, seen as a temporary 'tent' for the soul</note>, for Poimandres, the Mind of absolute authority, has handed down to me nothing more than what has been written. Knowing that by my own effort, I shall be able to conceive and hear all that I wish, and to see all things, if he acts well toward me. Therefore, in all things, the powers within me sing. I wish it; listen, and...

<meta>This page continues the secret discourse of Hermes Trismegistus to his son Tat concerning rebirth (palingenesia). Having discussed the transformation of the soul, Hermes now prepares to reveal the "Hymn of the Powers," a sacred song that celebrates the union of the soul with the divine All. The previous page ended with Hermes mentioning the "tabernacle" of the physical body being dissolved to reach the eighth sphere (the Ogdoad).</meta>

I wish you to understand, O Tat, O child, and listen to the blessing that is suitable for you. This hymn of **rebirth** <term>palingenesia; the spiritual process of being "born again" into a divine state</term> I did not think to reveal so easily, except to the one who exists within the All. God does not teach this openly, but hides it within Himself. 

Therefore, O child, stand in an open place facing the south wind <note>original: ὀρθῷ ἀνέμῳ (orthō anemō); literally the "straight" or "upright" wind, traditionally interpreted in Hermetic ritual as the South Wind</term>, gaze upon the setting sun and worship; do the same as it rises towards the east wind <note>original: ἀπηλιώτην (apēliōtēn); the wind from the sun/east</note>. Keep a holy silence, O child.

</margin>
**L**et the entire nature of the **world** <term>kosmos; the ordered universe</term> receive the hearing of this hymn. Open up, O earth! Let every bolt of the rain be opened to me! Trees, do not shake. We shall hymn the Lord of **creation** <term>ktisis</term>, both the All and the One. Open to me, you winds! Let the immortal circle of God receive my word. For I shall sing a hymn of creation to the All, to Him who fixed the earth and suspended the heaven, and who commanded sweet water to flow from the sweet water into the **inhabited world** <term>oikoumene</term> and the uninhabited regions, to provide nourishment and creation for all mankind. He is the one who commanded fire to appear for every purpose, for both gods and men. Let us all together give **blessings** <term>eulogia; words of praise</term> to Him who is high above the heavens, the creator of all nature.

<summary>Hermes instructs Tat on the ritual posture and silence required to hear the "Hymn of the Powers," which begins by calling upon the elements of nature to be still and witness the praise of the Creator.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, rebirth, palingenesia, ritual prayer, creation, elements, the One and the All</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Hymn of Rebirth" from the Corpus Hermeticum, Book XIII. Your **Mind** <term>nous; the divine intellect or higher consciousness</term> is light, the eye. May it receive the blessings of the Powers. The powers within me hymn the All and the All <note>original: τὸ πᾶν καὶ τὸ πᾶν (to pan kai to pan); an emphatic expression of the totality of the Divine</note>. Sing together with my will, all you powers within me. 

**Holy Knowledge** <term>gnosis</term>, having been enlightened by you, through you I hymn the intelligible light, and I rejoice in the joy of the Mind. All you powers, hymn with me. 

And you, sing for me, **Self-Control** <term>enkrateia</term>. My **Justice** hymns the Just through me. My **Fellowship** hymns the All. Through me, **Truth** hymns the Truth. The **Good** hymns the Good.

Your **Word** <term>logos</term> hymns you through me.
